# REDISCOVERING INDIA <note>A series title dedicated to the preservation and dissemination of classical Indian heritage and literature.</note>

# THE GARUDA PURANA <term>Garuda Purana: One of the eighteen "Great Puranas" or Mahapuranas of Hinduism. It is famously known for its dialogues between the god Vishnu and the bird-deity Garuda, covering topics from cosmology and ethics to funeral rites and the afterlife.</term>

STATE CENTRAL LIBRARY
( HYD. )
A. P.
</insert>

->DUTT M.N.<-
<note>Manmatha Nath Dutt (1855–1912) was a prolific scholar and translator who made many ancient Sanskrit texts accessible to English speakers during the late 19th and early 20th centuries.</note>

->Vol. 7 (i)<-

->COSMO PUBLICATIONS<-
<note>A New Delhi-based publishing house known for its extensive reprints of historical, academic, and cultural volumes concerning South Asia.</note>

<summary>This page serves as the title page for Volume 7 (part i) of the Garuda Purana, translated by M.N. Dutt and published as part of the "Rediscovering India" series. It establishes the academic and historical context of the text as a major work of Sanskrit literature intended for a modern audience.</summary>

<summary>This Table of Contents details the Garuda Purana's shift from mythological narratives of sages and cosmic cycles (Manus) to a practical handbook on Dharma-Shastra (Law and Ethics). It covers the life stages of a Hindu, including education, householder duties, ancestral rites, and the legal codes of the sages Yajnavalkya, Parashara, and Vrihaspati.</summary>
<keywords>Manus, Cosmic Eras, Sage Ruchi, Ancestral Rites (Shraddha), Ritual Purity, Laws of Yajnavalkya, Dharma-Shastra, Vrihaspati Samhita, Ethics (Nitisara), Renunciants (Yatis), Atonement</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the detailed Table of Contents to the Preface of the volume. It establishes the historical and literary context of the Garuda Purana, comparing it to other major works in the Puranic canon.</meta>

## PREFACE.

A few preliminary remarks on the history, scope and contents of the <term>Garuda Purana</term> <gloss>Garuda Puranam</gloss> <note>One of the eighteen Mahapuranas, a genre of ancient Indian texts covering diverse topics from mythology to medicine.</note> may be necessary. The Garuda Purana may be safely described as a sister work to the <term>Agni Purana</term> <gloss>Agni Puranam</gloss>. Each of them treats of <term>Supreme Knowledge</term> <gloss>Para Vidya</gloss> and <term>Lower Knowledge</term> <gloss>Apara Vidya</gloss>—secular knowledge and metaphysical truths—and partakes more of the nature of a catechism of the then prevailing <term>Brahmanism</term> <note>A historical stage of Hinduism centered on the authority of the Vedas and the rituals conducted by the priestly class.</note>, or of what a <term>Brahmin</term> <gloss>Brahmana</gloss> was required to know at the time, than of the <term>Purana</term> <gloss>Ancient Tale</gloss> proper, at least if we may be allowed to look upon the *Ramayana* or the *Mahabharata* as the model of that class of literature. Superficially conforming to the rules of the <term>Five Junctions</term> <gloss>Pancha Sandhis</gloss> <note>The traditional structural divisions used in Sanskrit literary composition to organize a plot.</note>, etc., the Garuda Purana, like its sister work, reflects the collective knowledge of the Brahminical world at the time, and had its uses then as it has even now.

Without doing violence to the antiquarian instinct, we must say that it is quite futile to attempt to lay down the precise date of the composition of the Garuda Purana. Its name occurs in <term>Halayudha’s Everything for a Brahmin</term> <gloss>Halayudha's Brahmana Sarvasvam</gloss>. <insert>Chakrapani Datta</insert> <note>Original OCR: Chakrpani Dntta. A famous 11th-century commentator on Ayurvedic texts.</note> has quoted many a recipe from it, and the <term>Highest Duty of Vishnu</term> <gloss>Vishnu Dharmottaram</gloss>, according to several eminent authorities, originally formed a portion of the Garuda Purana. All these factors emphatically demonstrate the fact that the Garuda Purana was in existence even before the tenth century of the Christian Era. On the contrary, we have reasons to believe that hosts of <term>Ancient Tales</term> <gloss>Puranas</gloss> and <term>Secondary Tales</term> <gloss>Upapuranas</gloss> were composed in the age of Brahminical renascence, which immediately followed the overthrow of Buddhism in India. The Garuda Purana, like the Shiva, Padma and similar Puranas, were the exponents of this victorious Brahmanism, which, being inevitably divided into various sects, tried to invest the tutelary deity of each sect with—

<summary>This preface introduces the Garuda Purana as an encyclopedic work of both spiritual and secular knowledge, dating it to at least before the 10th century CE.</summary>
<keywords>Garuda Purana, Agni Purana, Para Vidya, Apara Vidya, Brahmanism, Halayudha, Chakrapani Datta, Vishnu Dharmottaram, Puranic literature, Indian history</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface, shifting from the general nature of the Garuda Purana to its historical context, specifically its relationship with Buddhist science and the specific medical knowledge regarding human anatomy.</meta>

the attributes of supreme divinity or <term>Brahma</term> <gloss>the ultimate reality or creator god</gloss>, and to equip its members with a complete code of rituals, law and other necessary informations regarding the incidents of every day life, subservient to, and in conformity with, the <term>Vedas</term> <gloss>the most ancient sacred scriptures of Hinduism</gloss> and the Vedic literature. Thus each schism or faction, or more correctly, each <term>Purana</term> <gloss>Ancient Narrative</gloss>, the scripture of each sect of special, tutelary divinities, became a new school of law, medicine and metaphysics, etc., re-instating the old errors of the Vedic literature, as if to ignore the many advanced truths and principles of the later day Buddhist science, and to confirm the victory of Brahmanism even in error and fallacy.

The description of the incidents of the life of <term>Buddha</term>, however meagre and incidental it might be, and the occurrence of the name of <term>Sushruta</term> <note>A legendary ancient Indian physician and surgeon, often regarded as the "Father of Surgery."</note> in the medical portion of the <term>Garuda Purana</term> leaves not the slightest doubt that its author was intimately acquainted with the Buddhist literature of the age, both medical and metaphysical. It is a settled fact of history that the *Sushruta Samhita*, at least the recension of the *Sushruta Samhita* by the Buddhist <term>Nagarjuna</term> <note>A highly influential Buddhist philosopher and scholar who is traditionally credited with refining and editing the primary surgical text of India.</note>, was written in the second century before the birth of Christ. Now, the *Sushruta Samhita* says that, the number of bones in the human body is three hundred. The <term>Vishnu Smriti</term> <gloss>Institutes of Vishnu</gloss> following the orthodox (Vedic) non-medical opinion on the subject gives it as three hundred and sixty-six.

We know that Nagarjuna, the Buddhist redactor of the *Sushruta Samhita*, mentioned in his recension of the work that there are "three hundred bones in the human organism, <note>Likely a printing error for 'organism'.</note> but the followers of the Vedas say that their number is three hundred and sixty" which tallies with the number given in the <term>Yajnavalkya Samhita</term> <note>An important legal and religious text of the Hindu tradition, likely composed between the 3rd and 5th centuries CE.</note>. The *Garuda Purana* gives the number as three hundred and sixty two—"The bones are said to be three hundred and sixty-two" <note>original Sanskrit: *Asthnám Duyadhikam Proktam Sashthyadhika S'atatrayam*</note>—a sort of compromise between the Vedic and the Buddhist <term>osteology</term> <gloss>the scientific study of bones</gloss>, or between the dictates of conscience, imperatively

<summary>The author argues that the Garuda Purana represents a Brahmanical attempt to integrate and occasionally overwrite advanced Buddhist scientific knowledge, specifically comparing conflicting anatomical bone counts across several ancient texts.</summary>

urging the man to state the whole truth, and the pride of conquest impelling him to set up a schism against truth. The author of the <term>Garuda Puranam</term> <gloss>an ancient Hindu scripture</gloss>, whoever he might be, must have been sufficiently familiar with the works of <term>Nagarjuna</term> <note>A pivotal Buddhist philosopher and medical authority.</note> and other Buddhistic Medical <term>Acharyas</term> <gloss>teachers or masters</gloss> so as to be fully convinced of the truth of their s<unclear>tate</unclear>ment, and attempted to make the <term>Vedic</term> <gloss>relating to the oldest Hindu scriptures</gloss> number of skeletal bones as near to the truth as possible. This fact serves to throw a new light upon the date of the composition of the <term>Garuda Puranam</term>. It unmistakably points to a period of history when the victorious <term>Brahmanism</term> <gloss>the early social and religious system of the Brahmins</gloss> once more attempted to restore the teachings of the <term>Vedas</term> in their pristine glory, and the truths of the Buddhistic science or metaphysics were still too potent a factor to be ignored or lightly dismissed—a fact which supports our contention and lends a plausible colour to the view we have adopted as regards the probable date of the composition of the Garuda.

The second question, that confronts us, is the purity of the text, *i. e.*, whether the <term>Garuda Puranam</term>, as we now possess it, is what it was originally written by its author; or whether its bulk has been considerably increased by subsequent additions? In the first Chapter we learn that, the <term>Puranam</term> consists of eight thousand and eight hundred verses, and the subjects dealt with therein are creation of the universe, <term>Pujas</term> <gloss>ritual worship</gloss>, Holy pools and shrines, Cosmogony and Geography, Ages of <term>Manus</term> <gloss>progenitors of humanity</gloss>, Duties of different social orders, Gift-making, Duties of kings, etc., Laws, <term>Vratas</term> <gloss>religious vows</gloss>, Royal dynasties, Therapeutics with Ætiology <note>the study of the causes of diseases</note>, <term>Vedangas</term> <gloss>limbs of the Veda; auxiliary disciplines</gloss>, <term>Pralaya</term> <gloss>cosmic dissolution</gloss>, Laws of Virtue, desire, and money, and Knowledge (of <term>Brahma</term> <gloss>the ultimate reality</gloss> and external things). These then were the main themes that were <unclear>o</unclear>riginally dealt with in the <term>Garuda Puranam</term>, and we may say that this was so in the light of the principle of <term>Adhyaya Sampravibhága</term> <gloss>the systematic classification of chapters</gloss>, which forms one of the cardinal rules in forming the plan of a <term>Sanskrit</term> work. We regret to say that, many things, having no legitimate connection with the main themes of this <term>Puranam</term>, nor having a direct bearing thereon,

have been added to it, and a large mass of original matter has been expunged from it so as to bring it within the compass of the eight thousand and eight hundred <term>Slokas</term> <gloss>metrical verses</gloss>, as laid down in the introductory chapter. Thus we see that the <term>Pretakhanda</term> <gloss>the section concerning the departed spirits and the afterlife</gloss> or <term>Vishnu-dharmottara</term> <gloss>a supplementary treatise on the laws and rituals of Vishnu</gloss> was added to it by way of an appendix, and the reason of these successive accretions to the text can be easily understood if we consider that, the <term>Garuda Puranam</term> <gloss>one of the eighteen great Hindu scriptures</gloss>, like the <term>Agni</term> <note>referring to the Agni Purana</note>, etc. although originally a compendium of the available Brahminical knowledge, and rituals, pursued and followed by the <term>Vaishnava</term> <gloss>followers of the god Vishnu</gloss> section of the community, came to gather in many tributaries from the other branches of Brahmanic thought and religion, as the distinction between the sect of <term>Vishnu</term> and other sects of <term>S'iva</term> <gloss>Shiva</gloss> and <term>Sakti</term> <gloss>Shakti, or the Divine Mother</gloss> etc., came to be less marked and pronounced, and the points of difference or antagonism between them were more rounded off. 

Thus we see many <term>Tantrik</term> <gloss>esoteric or ritualistic</gloss> rites and <term>Mantras</term> <gloss>sacred chants or formulas</gloss> such as, the <term>*Tripurâ Vidyâ*</term> <gloss>The Sacred Knowledge of the Three Cities</gloss>, <term>*Nityaklinnâ Vidyâ*</term> <gloss>The Sacred Rite of the Ever-Moist One</gloss> were introduced into the <term>Garuda Puranam</term>, one of the Scriptural Puranas of Vaishnavism; and the <term>*Preta-khanda*</term>, which we find invariably appended to the Purana in many of the manuscripts, does but reflect the necessity of subsequently adding to it a treatise on funeral rites, or on punishment and reward after death according to one's deserts, only to enhance the utility of the work as a book of reference in every day life, as the members of the sect began to be more bigoted and averse to reading religious works, or Puranas dedicated to the tutelary gods of other sects. 

It requires nothing more than an average intellect to detect that the part under reference (<term>Preta-Khanda</term>) is manifestly an interpolation <note>a later addition not part of the original text</note>, inasmuch as the subject has been already dealt with in chapters on <term>*S'raddha-vidhi*</term> <gloss>rules for ancestral funeral rites</gloss>, <term>*Papa-Chinha Lakshanam*</term> <gloss>the study of the marks and signs of sin</gloss> and <term>*Prayaschittâ*</term> <gloss>atonement or penance</gloss>, etc., and the insertion of a more detailed and elaborate dissertation on the subject under the style of <term>*Preta-Khanda*</term> is an unnecessary repetition and re-opening of a finished discourse (<term>*Samâpta Punarâtta-tâ*</term> <gloss>the rhetorical fault of resuming a concluded topic</gloss>) which is bad both in reason and rhetoric. We have

The author argues that the Pretakhanda is a later addition because it repeats topics like funeral rites and penance that were already addressed earlier in the text.</summary>

<page-num>v</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

# PREFACE.

...attempted to expunge all spurious <note>Spurious refers to portions that are not original or are of questionable authenticity, often added by later scribes.</note> portions, or passages of questionable authenticity from the text in the light of the reasons stated above, and tried to restore it to its original form as far as possible after the progress of so many centuries since it first saw the light.

We may be asked the rationale of our conduct in undertaking the English translation of the <term>Garuda Puranam</term> <gloss>one of the eighteen major Hindu religious texts, traditionally delivered by the divine bird Garuda to the sage Kashyapa</gloss>. The question is natural enough; if the work is nothing but a compendium of Brahmanic rituals and mysteries, what is the profit of disinterring it from beneath the oblivion which it so unqualifiedly deserves? Our answer is that, in addition to the many mystic rites and practices, which legitimately fall within the range of studies in spiritualism, the <term>Garuda Puranam</term> contains three <term>Samhitas</term> <gloss>systematic collections of knowledge or treatises</gloss>, *viz.*, the <term>Agastya Samhita</term>, the <term>Brihaspati Samhita</term> (<term>Nitisara</term> <gloss>The Essence of Conduct or Ethics</gloss>), and the <term>Dhanvantari Samhita</term>; any one of which would give it a permanent value, and accord to it an undying fame among the works of practical Ethics or applied medicine. The <term>Agastya Samhita</term> deals with the formation, crystallisation and distinctive traits of the different precious jems <note>An archaic spelling of "gems."</note>, and enumerates the names of the countries from which our forefathers used to collect those minerals. The cutting, polishing, setting, and appraising, etc., of the several kinds of jems and diamond, as they were practised in ancient India, can not but be interesting to artists and lay men alike, and the scientific truths, imbedded in the highly poetic accounts of their origin and formation, shall, we doubt not, be welcomed even by the present day mineralogists, if they only care to look through the veil and to see them in their pure and native nuditity <note>Likely a typo for "nudity," meaning the raw or unadorned state of the facts.</note>. In these days of Oriental research, it is quite within the possibilities of every ardent enquirer to make himself acquainted with the terms and technicalities of the science of our <term>Rishis</term> <gloss>the inspired sages or seers who composed the Vedic hymns and scientific treatises</gloss>, and we are confident that any labour he may bestow on the subject in connection with the <term>Agastya Samhita</term> will be remunerated a hundred-fold.

The next <term>Samhita</term> in the <term>Garuda Puranam</term> is the <term>Brihaspati</term>

<page-num>vi</page-num>
<header>PREFACE</header>

<term>Samhita</term> <gloss>a systematic collection of knowledge</gloss>, commonly known as the <term>Nitisara</term> <gloss>The Essence of Conduct or Ethics</gloss>, in which we find observations on practical conduct and a knowledge of human nature, which strongly remind one of Bacon's essays <note>Referring to the 16th-century English philosopher Francis Bacon, known for his pragmatic and analytical essays on human behavior.</note> and in comparison with which the <term>Samhita</term> gains one or two points more, not to speak of its excellent poetry and harmony. In the <term>Etiological</term> <gloss>relating to the causes of disease</gloss> portion of the <term>Dhanvantari Samhita</term> <gloss>a medical treatise named after the celestial physician of the gods</gloss>, one is astonished to find that in "certain types of fever the blood undergoes a sort of chemical change which produces the <term>morbific factors</term> <gloss>elements that generate disease</gloss> of the disease, that in <term>hæmoptisis</term> <gloss>the coughing up of blood</gloss> the blood comes from the spleen, liver or the blood-vessels (facts unknown to the <term>Nidanist</term> <gloss>pathologist or specialist in diagnosis</gloss>, <term>Madhava</term> <gloss>a famous 7th-century Indian physician known for his work on pathology</gloss>), that there is a kind of parasites that produces leprosy, and cutaneous affections in general," facts which, it was but yesterday, that the science of the west have gained access to. The therapeutical portion of the <term>Samhita</term> contains many excellent remedies which can not but benefit man in the art of living a long, healthy life. It is almost impossible for us to give <unclear>within</unclear> such a small compass even the faintest glimpse of the splendid truths that lie scattered through the pages of this noble <term>Puranam</term> <gloss>an ancient Indian encyclopedic text</gloss>; enough if we conclude our remark with the saying that, it broadens the vision of a man into regions where systems and worlds are but bubbles and atoms, and enables him to consolidate his amity with those profound realities, which encompass "being and becoming" in every plane of existence, or at least helps him to lift up the veil of the Nature's workshop and to catch a view, however slight and momentary, of the nature and essence of things.

->CALCUTTA,<-

->The 12th June, 1908<-

->M. N. DUTT<-

-># THE<-
-># GARUDA PURANA<-
<note>original: "Garuda Puranam." This text is one of the eighteen "Mahapuranas" (Great Ancient Chronicles) of Hinduism, traditionally attributed to the sage Vyasa. It is famous for its discourse on the soul, death, and the afterlife, though it covers a vast range of topics including medicine and ethics.</note>

->## CHAPTER I<-

---

I salute the One Supreme, without birth, decay, or end, identical with knowledge, great, auspicious, free from impurities, without beginning, devoid of an elemental body and actions, residing within all creatures, <term>Hari</term> <gloss>a name for Vishnu, meaning "The Remover of Sin"</gloss>, freed from impurity and illusion and present everywhere. With mind, speech, and actions I do always salute <term>Hari</term>, <term>Rudra</term> <gloss>a fierce form of Shiva</gloss>, <term>Brahma</term> <gloss>the Creator deity</gloss>, the lord of <term>Ganas</term> <gloss>Ganesha, the elephant-headed god who leads the divine attendants</gloss> and the goddess <term>Saraswati</term> <gloss>the goddess of wisdom, music, and the arts</gloss>. 

Having adored and praised the poet <term>Suta</term> <gloss>traditionally identified as Lomaharshana, the bard who recited the Puranas to the sages</gloss>, who is well-versed in the <term>Puranas</term> <gloss>ancient lore and history</gloss>, of a quiet nature, the master of all scriptures, devoted to <term>Vishnu</term> and high-souled—when he came to the forest of <term>Naimisha</term> <note>A sacred forest frequently mentioned in Indian epics as a place for spiritual retreats and the performance of long-term sacrifices.</note> while making a pilgrimage to sacred shrines and, seated on a holy seat, was meditating on the sinless Vishnu—the great <term>Rishis</term> <gloss>sages or seers</gloss>, <term>Shaunaka</term> and others, dwelling in the forest of <term>Naimishi</term> <note>The text uses both "Naimisha" and "Naimishi" interchangeably for the same sacred site.</note>, having asceticism for their wealth, of quiescent souls, radiant like the sun and ever engaged in the celebration of <unclear>s</unclear>acrifices, said.

The <term>Rishis</term> said: — "O <term>Suta</term>, thou art informed of everything and therefore we ask you: — 'Amongst the celestials...'"

<meta>This page continues the introduction to the Garuda Purana. It picks up as the gathered sages in the Naimisha forest pose a series of foundational theological questions to the narrator, Suta, who then begins to recount the history of the text and the early incarnations of Lord Vishnu.</meta>

### GARUDA PURANA

Who is <term>Ishvara</term> <gloss>the Supreme Lord and Controller</gloss> and who is worthy of adoration? Who should be meditated upon? Who is the creator of the universe? Who protects it and who destroys it? From whom proceeds <term>Dharma</term> <gloss>universal law and righteousness</gloss>? Who suppresses the wicked? With what vowed observances is He pleased? By what <term>Yoga</term> <gloss>spiritual discipline or union</gloss> can He be obtained? What are His incarnations and what is His lineage? Who has instituted the various castes and orders of life and who protects them? O <term>Suta</term>, O thou of great intellect, do thou describe unto us all this and everything else—the most excellent themes about <term>Narayana</term> <gloss>a name for Vishnu, meaning "He who dwells in the waters" or "The Refuge of Men"</gloss>.

**SUTA said:**—I will describe the <term>Garuda Purana</term>, the essence of all accounts relating to <term>Vishnu</term>. Formerly, <term>Garuda</term> <gloss>the divine eagle-vehicle of Vishnu</gloss> described it to <term>Kashyapa</term> <gloss>an ancient progenitor sage</gloss> and I myself had heard it from <term>Vyasa</term> <gloss>the legendary sage who compiled the Vedas and Puranas</gloss>. 

The Lord <term>Narayana</term> alone is the lord of all the lords of the deities, the Great Soul and the Great <term>Brahma</term> <gloss>the ultimate, impersonal reality</gloss>; from Him proceeds birth and all existence. For the protection of the universe, <term>Vasudeva</term> <gloss>Vishnu in his omnipresent form</gloss>, who is without birth or death, assumes various incarnations through his divine form, namely that of <term>Kaumara</term> <gloss>the "Ever-Youthful" boy-sages</gloss> and others. 

Having undertaken the incarnation of <term>Kaumara</term> and lived in the celestial region, the first deity <term>Hari</term> <gloss>a name for Vishnu meaning "The Remover"</gloss>, O <term>Brahman</term> <gloss>Priest or Sage</gloss>, practiced the most difficult vow of celibacy without any break. 

Secondly, having assumed the <term>Varaha</term> <gloss>the Boar-form</gloss>, the Lord of Sacrifices, for the general well-being of all, released and held up the earth which had sunk deep into the nether region. 

Thirdly, for the creation of the <term>Rishis</term> <gloss>Seers</gloss>, He assumed the form of a <term>Brahma Rishi</term> and performed <term>Sattvic</term> actions <gloss>actions pervaded by the quality of goodness, purity, and harmony</gloss> from which alone proceed desireless actions. 

Then, for protecting righteousness, <term>Hari</term>, assuming the form of <term>Nara-Narayana</term> <gloss>the twin-sage incarnation representing the human soul and its divine companion</gloss>, practiced hard penances. He was then adored by the celestials and the <term>Asuras</term> <gloss>demonic or titanic beings who often struggle against the gods</gloss>. 

His fifth incarnation was <term>Kapila</term>, the lord of the <term>Siddhas</term> <note>Siddhas are semi-divine, inspired seers of great purity and holiness. They are characterized by having attained "Siddhis," or eight supernatural faculties and perfections.</note> who expounded unto—

<meta>This page continues the list of the twenty-four incarnations of Lord Vishnu. The previous page ended mid-sentence during the description of the fourth or fifth incarnation. This text picks up with the fifth incarnation, as the sage Kapila reveals the Sankhya philosophy.</meta>

# GARUDA PURANA

...to the <term>Devas</term> <gloss>celestial beings or gods</gloss> the <term>Sankhya</term> <gloss>an ancient system of philosophy based on the enumeration of cosmic principles</gloss>,* which ascertains the <term>Tattwas</term> <gloss>the fundamental elements or true nature of reality</gloss> which had been lost to time. In his sixth incarnation, He was born as the son of the sage <term>Atri</term> and his wife <term>Anasuya</term>; in this form, He communicated the knowledge of <term>Anvikshiki</term> <gloss>the science of logic and metaphysics</gloss> to <term>Alarka</term>, <term>Prahlada</term>, and others. 

His seventh incarnation, occurring during the <term>Svayambhuva Manvantara</term> <gloss>the vast cosmic age of the first Manu, or progenitor of humanity</gloss>, was <term>Yajna</term> <gloss>the personification of Sacrifice</gloss>. He was the son of <term>Akuti</term> and performed sacred rites alongside the other celestial beings. 

In His eighth incarnation, He was born as the powerful son of King <term>Nabhi</term> and Queen <term>Merudevi</term>. Adored by all social orders, He pointed out the true path of spiritual life to mankind <note>original: "pointed out to men the real road"</note>. Solicited by the <term>Rishis</term> <gloss>divinely inspired sages</gloss>, He assumed the body of King <term>Prithu</term> as His ninth incarnation; through him, the <term>Brahmins</term> <gloss>priests and scholars</gloss> and all other creatures were sustained by the "milk" of the earth's herbs <note>This refers to the legend where King Prithu leveled the earth and milked it in the form of a cow to provide nourishment for all living things.</note>. 

When the Earth was submerged under water at the end of the <term>Chakshusha Manvantara</term> <gloss>the sixth cosmic age</gloss>, He assumed the form of a **Fish** and saved the <term>Manu Vaivasvata</term> <gloss>the progenitor of the current human race</gloss> by guiding his boat through the deluge. 

In His eleventh incarnation, the Lord, in the shape of a **Tortoise**, held the mountain <term>Mandara</term> upon His back to serve as a pivot while the gods and the <term>Asuras</term> <gloss>titans or demonic rivals of the gods</gloss> churned the great ocean to obtain the nectar of immortality. 

In His twelfth incarnation, as well as in the thirteenth—where He took the form of an enchanting **Woman** <gloss>known as Mohini</gloss>—He deluded the <term>Asuras</term> and brought joy to the celestial gods. 

Assuming the form of a **Man-lion** in His fourteenth incarnation, He subdued the chief of the <term>Daityas</term> <gloss>a race of giants</gloss> by tearing open his chest with His fierce claws. 

Assuming the form of a **Dwarf** in His fifteenth incarnation, He attended the sacrifice of King <term>Bali</term>; there, He requested only as much ground as He could cover in three steps, and through those steps, He reclaimed the universe. 

Beholding how the kings of the earth began to oppress the <term>Brahmins</term> <note>original: "aggrandise the Brahmanas" — likely meaning the kings grew arrogant and overstepped their bounds against the priestly order</note> in His sixteenth incarnation, He, filled with righteous anger, cleared the earth of the <term>Kshatriyas</term> <gloss>the warrior and ruling caste</gloss> twenty-one times. 

In His seventeenth incarnation, He was fathered by the sage <term>Parashara</term> upon <term>Satyavati</term> as the sage <term>Veda-Vyasa</term>. Observing that the intellect of people was becoming weak in the coming age, He divided the single <term>Veda</term> into multiple parts for easier study. Then, for the purpose of accomplishing...

---

* <note>original note: It is a system of philosophy ascribed to Kapila. It is called Sankhya because it enumerates twenty-five Tattwas or principles. According to this system final liberation is obtained by a true knowledge of these principles.</note>

# GARUDA PURANA

...to further the work of the <term>Devas</term> <gloss>the celestial beings or gods</gloss>, He was born as a king—<term>Rama</term>—who built a bridge over the ocean and performed other miraculous feats. In His nineteenth and twentieth incarnations, the Lord was born as <term>Balarama</term> <note>The text simply says "Rama" here, referring to Krishna's elder brother to distinguish him from the previous Rama.</note> and <term>Krishna</term>, and thereby relieved the earth of her heavy burden. Then, at the junction of the <term>Kali Yuga</term> <gloss>the current age of spiritual darkness and discord</gloss>, for the purpose of confusing the enemies of the gods, He was born as <term>Buddha</term>, the son of <term>Jina</term> <note>original: "Jina"; in Puranic accounts, Buddha is sometimes referred to as the son of Ajina or associated with the lineage of the "Jinas" or conquerors.</note>. In the period of the eighth junction <gloss>the transition between cosmic cycles</gloss>, when all the kings will be on the verge of destruction, He will be born as <term>Kalki</term>, the son of <term>Vishnuyasa</term> <note>original: "Vishnujasa"</note>, the lord of the world. 

Innumerable are the incarnations of <term>Hari</term> <gloss>a popular name for Vishnu, meaning "The Remover of Sin"</gloss>, who is the very mine of <term>Sattva</term> <gloss>the quality of goodness, purity, and harmony</gloss>, O twice-born ones <note>A term for those who have undergone spiritual initiation, typically the priestly or warrior classes.</note>. The prime <term>Manus</term> <gloss>the ancestral progenitors of humanity</gloss> and the knowers of the <term>Vedas</term> <gloss>the most ancient and sacred scriptures</gloss> are all said to have originated from <term>Vishnu</term>. From them proceeded the work of creation. And they should be adored with vowed observances and other sacred rites. This *Garuda Purana*, consisting of eight thousand and eight hundred verses, was narrated to me in former times by the sage <term>Vyasa</term>.

**THE RISHIS** <gloss>the forest-dwelling sages</gloss> said:—Why did <term>Vyasa</term> describe the *Garuda Purana* to you? Please describe that most excellent theme relating to <term>Vishnu</term>.

**THE SUTA** <gloss>the traditional title for a bard or storyteller</gloss> said:—In the company of the ascetics, I had gone to the hermitage of <term>Badarika</term> <note>original: "Vadarika"; known today as Badrinath, a sacred site in the Himalayas.</note>. There I saw <term>Vyasa</term> meditating on the great <term>Ishwara</term> <gloss>the Supreme Lord</gloss>. Having bowed unto him, I took my seat and addressed that foremost of ascetics, saying:—

# GARUDA PURANA

Hear, O <term>Vipras</term> <gloss>learned priests</gloss>, what he related when I approached him with these questions.

**VYASA said:** — Hear, O <term>Suta</term> <gloss>the narrator and bard of the Puranas</gloss>, I will recount the Garuda Purana which <term>Brahma</term> <gloss>the Creator</gloss> described to me in the company of <term>Narada</term>, <term>Daksha</term>, and others.

**THE SUTA said:** — How did Brahma relate the sacred Garuda Purana—which describes the true essence of reality—unto you when you were united with Daksha, Narada, and the others?

**VYASA said:** — Having offered our respects to Brahma in <term>Brahmaloka</term> <gloss>the highest heavenly realm</gloss>, myself, Daksha, Narada, <term>Bhrigu</term>, and others said to him: "Describe to us the very heart of all sacred wisdom."

**BRAHMA said:** — O Vyasa, I will recount to you the <term>Garuda Purana</term> <note>The original printed OCR contains a typo here: "Purada" for "Purana."</note>, the very cream of sacred learning which <term>Vishnu</term> <gloss>the Preserver</gloss> described to me and <term>Rudra</term> <gloss>an aspect of Shiva</gloss> in the past, while in the company of other celestial beings.

**VYASA said:** — O <term>Brahman</term> <gloss>the Creator</gloss>, describe to me this Garuda Purana, which is full of great significance and is the essence of all things—the same which <term>Hari</term> <gloss>a name for Vishnu</gloss> formerly described to Rudra while in the company of other gods.

**BRAHMA said:** — Along with <term>Indra</term> <gloss>the King of the Gods</gloss> and other celestials, I traveled to <term>Mount Kailasha</term> <gloss>the sacred mountain home of Shiva</gloss>. There I saw Rudra engaged in the meditation of the "Great Station" <note>A term referring to the supreme state of consciousness or the Ultimate Reality.</note>. Having saluted him, I addressed him, saying: "O <term>Shankara</term> <gloss>the Giver of Joy, a title of Shiva</gloss>, upon what are you meditating? Aside from you, I know of no greater deity. Therefore, please describe to me the subject of your meditation—the very essence of sacred learning—for I, along with these gods, am anxious to listen to it."

**RUDRA said:** — I meditate on Vishnu, the Great Soul, the <term>Demiurgus</term> <gloss>the divine architect or creator of the universe</gloss>, who provides everything, who is present everywhere, and who resides in the hearts of all living creatures. O Grandfather <note>A traditional honorific for Brahma as the progenitor of all beings.</note>, though my body is smeared with ashes and my hair is matted, all my religious vows are intended for the adoration of Vishnu. I will describe Him to you—the essence of all—upon whom I meditate. I meditate on the deity...

<summary>This page establishes the divine origins of the Garuda Purana, tracing its transmission from Vishnu to Shiva and Brahma, then to the sage Vyasa. It emphasizes the supreme position of Vishnu, revealing that even Shiva (Rudra) spends his time in deep meditation upon Him.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Brahma’s discourse to the sages. Having agreed to share the essence of the Garuda Purana, Brahma begins by offering a profound hymn of praise to Vishnu (Hari), describing his various names, his transcendent nature, and his role as the source and substance of the entire universe.</meta>

### GARUDA PURANA

<term>Hari</term> <gloss>the Remover of Sorrows</gloss>, <term>Vishnu</term> <gloss>the Pervader</gloss>, and <term>Jishnu</term> <gloss>the Victorious</gloss>—he is the one with the lotus-navel and is without a physical body. He is purity itself and the very source of all purification. He is both the impersonal, supreme Self and the individual soul; he is the great <term>Ishwara</term> <gloss>Supreme Lord</gloss> who unites all souls within himself. In him, all worlds and elements exist, and into him, they eventually return. All the <term>gunas</term> <gloss>the fundamental qualities of nature</gloss> and the elements exist within that Lord of elements, just as pearls are held together by a single thread. 

He possesses a thousand eyes, a thousand heads, a thousand thighs, and a most beautiful face. He is the smallest of the small, the firmest of the firm, the heaviest of the heavy, and the finest of all that is good. In sacred words, letters, and both the primary and secondary holy scriptures, and in the true <term>Saman</term> <gloss>the chants of the Sama Veda</gloss>, he is praised as the ultimate Truth and the author of all true deeds. 

He is called the ancient <term>Purusha</term> <gloss>the Cosmic Person</gloss> and is known as <term>Brahma</term> <gloss>the Creator</gloss> among the "twice-born" <note>This refers to the *dvija*, or members of the three upper social classes who have undergone spiritual initiation.</note>. In the act of destruction, he is called <term>Sankarshana</term> <gloss>the aspect of the Divine that draws the universe back into himself</gloss>. I therefore adore him, in whom all these worlds shine just as the <term>Shakula</term> fishes <gloss>a type of trout or carp mentioned in ancient texts</gloss> glimmer in the water. 

He is the Divine Law, the sacred syllable <term>Om</term>, the Creator, the "That" <note>original: "Tat." A reference to the ultimate, indescribable reality.</note>, the existent and the non-existent, and the Supreme. The celestials, the <term>Yakshas</term> <gloss>nature spirits</gloss>, the <term>Rakshasas</term> <note>The original OCR reads "Kakshasas," likely a transcription error for Rakshasas, or guardian/demonic spirits.</note>, and the <term>Pannagas</term> <gloss>serpent-deities</gloss> all adore him. 

Fire is his mouth, the heavens are his head, the sky is his navel, and the earth constitutes his feet; the sun and moon are his two eyes. I meditate upon him. I meditate on that deity whose breath is the wind, in whose belly the three worlds exist, and whose arms are the very divisions of time. I meditate on that deity in whose hair the clouds reside, and in the joints of whose body the four oceans are found. I meditate on that deity who exists beyond time, beyond sacrifice, and beyond the concepts of existence and non-existence—he who is the beginning and the end of the universe. 

I meditate on that deity from whose mind the moon was born, from whose eyes the sun emerged, and from whose mouth fire was produced. I meditate on that deity from whose feet the earth was created, from whose ears the cardinal directions were formed, and from whose head the <term>Div</term> <gloss>the celestial or heavenly realm</gloss> originated. I meditate on that deity from whom have proceeded the works of primary and secondary creation, the various races of beings, the <term>Manwantaras</term> <gloss>the vast cyclical ages of humanity</gloss>, and the histories of the great families.

<summary>This page features a majestic hymn to Vishnu, identifying him as the cosmic source from whose body the physical universe—including the sun, moon, and elements—is formed. It establishes Vishnu's role as the supreme, all-encompassing reality that precedes and survives all of creation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discourse of Brahma, shifting from his initial hymn of praise to a foundational dialogue. # GARUDA PURANA

We shall draw near to him to witness the true essence—he upon whom I meditate.

**BRAHMA** said: Long ago, I was addressed in this way by **Rudra** <gloss>an intense and powerful aspect of Shiva</gloss>. After we had offered our praise and saluted **Vishnu**, the inhabitant of the White Island <note>original: *Shweta-dwipa*; in Hindu cosmology, this is a celestial abode of pure light where Vishnu resides</note>, we sat down, calmly desiring to listen to him. From among us, **Rudra** spoke to **Vishnu**, the great <term>Ishwara</term> <gloss>Supreme Lord</gloss>: “Describe to us the most essential of all essences; we bow to you.” What you have asked me now, O **Vyasa** <note>The legendary sage and compiler of the Puranas to whom Brahma is speaking</note>, is exactly what Lord **Bhava** <gloss>another name for Shiva, meaning 'the Source of Being'</gloss> asked **Vishnu** in the presence of myself and the other celestial beings.

**RUDRA** said: O **Hari** <gloss>the Remover of Sorrows</gloss>, O King of the Gods, tell us: who is the God of gods? Who is the <term>Ishwara</term>? Who is the proper object of our meditation and our worship? Through what sacred vows is the Great One satisfied? Through what religious practices, what methods of self-restraint, what forms of adoration, and what kind of conduct is He pleased? What is His true form? From which deity did this universe emerge, and who protects it? What are His incarnations? Into whom is the world eventually dissolved? From which deity do the works of primary and secondary creation, the various families of beings, and the <term>Manvantaras</term> <gloss>the vast cosmic epochs, each ruled by a different 'Manu' or progenitor of humanity</gloss> proceed? In whom do all these things exist? Please describe all this and everything else to us, O **Hari**.

Then **Hari** described to **Rudra** the glories of the great <term>Ishwara</term>, the practice of <term>Yoga</term> <gloss>spiritual discipline and union</gloss>, and the eighteen branches of learning <note>These traditionally include the four Vedas, the six limbs of the Vedas, and various systems of logic, law, and history</note>.

**HARI** said: Listen, O **Rudra**, and I will describe it to you, as well as to **Brahma** and the other celestial beings.

I am the God of gods, the Lord of all the worlds. I am the object of meditation and adoration, and I am praised by the celestials with hymns of glory. When I am worshipped by humanity through self-restraint, sacred vows, and righteous conduct, O **Rudra**, I grant them the highest state of existence.

I am the seed from which the world is preserved, and I am also the destroyer of the universe, O **Shiva**. O **Hara** <gloss>the Seizer or Destroyer</gloss>, I am the one who suppresses the wicked and protects religion. Through the Fish <gloss>the Matsya avatar</gloss> and my other incarnations, I protect the—

...earth. I am the inner meaning of every sacred mantra and I am devoted to adoration and meditation. I am the creator of the heavens and I am the heavens themselves. I am the one who knows, the one who hears, the one who speaks, and the very object of all speech. I am everything, and I am the deity who is identical with all of existence. I am the source of both worldly enjoyments and spiritual liberation <note>original: *mukti*; the release from the cycle of rebirth</note>. I am the fruits of meditation and prayer; I am the <term>Mandalas</term> <gloss>sacred geometric diagrams used for focus</gloss> and the <term>Itihasas</term> <gloss>epic histories, such as the Mahabharata</gloss>, O **Rudra**. I am **Brahma**, all the celestial regions, and I am identical with every divinity. I am the personification of righteous conduct and the path of **Vishnu**. I am the <term>Varnas</term> <gloss>the four social classes</gloss> and the <term>Ashramas</term> <gloss>the four stages of life</gloss>, and I am the ancient religion. I am the process of self-discipline, the path of self-restraint, and the various sacred vows, O **Rudra**. I am the sun, the moon, and all auspicious things. Long ago, through intense spiritual discipline, the bird **Garuda** worshipped me on earth. Pleased with him, I invited him to ask for a blessing; and he made his request.

**GARUDA** said:—O **Hari**, my mother **Vinata** has been made a slave by the <term>Nagas</term> <gloss>the divine serpent race</gloss>. Grant that I may bring the nectar of immortality <note>original: *Amrita*; the drink of the gods that grants eternal life</note> after defeating the dwellers of heaven, so that I may release her from slavery and become your own mount <note>original: *Vahana*; the animal vehicle of a deity</note>. Grant also that I may be exceptionally powerful, immensely strong, all-knowing, the subduer of the **Nagas**, and the author of **Puranas** <gloss>ancient chronicles</gloss> and <term>Samhitas</term> <gloss>sacred collections of hymns</gloss>.

**VISHNU** said:—Everything you have asked, O **Garuda**, shall be fulfilled. You will free your mother **Vinata** from the **Nagas**. After defeating all the deities and others, you will bring the nectar of immortality. You will be my immensely powerful mount. By my favor, you will be the author of the **Purana** that describes my glories and forms. It will be celebrated on earth by your name as the **Garuda Purana**. O son of **Vinata**, just as I am the God of gods and as **Shri** <note>original: *Shree*; the goddess of fortune and consort of Vishnu</note> is celebrated, so too shall the **Garuda Purana** be celebrated among all the **Puranas**. As I am...

### CHAPTER III.

->—————<-

<term>SUTA</term> <gloss>the traditional title for a narrator of the Puranas</gloss> said:—This is how <term>Rudra</term> heard it from <term>Vishnu</term>; <term>Brahmā</term> <gloss>the Creator deity</gloss> heard it from **Rudra**; the ascetic <term>Vyasa</term> <gloss>the legendary sage credited with compiling the Vedas and Puranas</gloss> heard it from **Brahmā**; and I myself heard it from **Vyasa**. I now relate it to you, O <term>Shounaka</term> <gloss>the leader of the sages in the forest</gloss>, in the forest of <term>Naimisha</term> <gloss>a sacred forest where sages gathered to perform long-term sacrifices and hear sacred histories</gloss>.

In this gathering of ascetics, I have described the creation and the worship of the deities, the various sacred shrines, the riches of the world, and the <term>Manwantaras</term> <gloss>vast cosmic time cycles, each lasting millions of years and ruled by a different Manu or progenitor</gloss>. I have described the duties of the various <term>Varnas</term> <gloss>social classes</gloss> and <term>Ashramas</term> <gloss>the four stages of life, such as student and householder</gloss>; the practice of giving, the duties of kings, the laws, sacred vows, the history of families, and the science of medicine including the diagnosis of diseases. I have also detailed the various supporting sciences, the final dissolution of the universe, religious merit, desire, worldly success, and the most supreme knowledge.

The illusory acts of <term>Vishnu</term>, as well as those deeds that exist beyond the reach of illusion, have all been narrated in this **Garuda Purana**. This **Garuda** is <term>Bhagavan</term> <note>A title for a deity or a highly realized being. It literally refers to one who possesses six divine attributes: prosperity, power, glory, beauty, wisdom, and renunciation.</note>. By the grace of <term>Vāsudeva</term> <gloss>a name for Vishnu meaning "the indwelling Lord"</gloss>, he has been granted immense power. Having become the...

<page-num>10</page-num>
<header>GARUDA PURANAM.</header>

As the carrier of **Hari** <gloss>a name for Vishnu meaning "He who takes away" sins</gloss>, he is the instrument of creation and other cosmic works. Having defeated the <term>Celestials</term> <gloss>the devas or gods of heaven</gloss>, **Garuda** brought back the <term>Ambrosia</term> <gloss>original: amrita; the nectar of immortality</gloss>, which satisfied the hunger of the universe that lies within the belly of **Hari**. The mere sight or memory of him destroys serpents. Through the power of **Garuda**, the sage **Kashyapa** <note>the father of both the gods and the birds</note> restored life to all the trees that had been burnt to ashes. **Garuda** is essentially **Hari** himself; he related this knowledge to **Kashyapa**. This auspicious **Garuda Purana**, when read, grants piety and fulfills all desires. Hear now, O **Shounaka**, how **Hari** described it to **Rudra**.

### CHAPTER IV.

---

**RUDRA** <gloss>Shiva</gloss> said:—O **Janarddana** <note>original: Janārddana; a title for Vishnu meaning "the agitator of men" or "he who is prayed to for liberation"</note>, please describe the primary and secondary stages of creation, the royal lineages, the <term>Manwantaras</term> <gloss>the vast cycles of time, each ruled by a different progenitor of humanity</gloss>, and the histories of those great families.

**HARI** <gloss>Vishnu</gloss> said:—Hear, O **Rudra**, for I will describe the ancient, divine play of **Vishnu**. It consists of the creation, preservation, and destruction of the world, and it has the power to destroy all sins.

The Lord **Vasudeva** <note>the son of Vasudeva, referring to the incarnate Vishnu/Krishna</note>, who is free from all passion, is **Nara-Narayana** <note>the twin-sage avatar representing the perfect human and the divine spirit</note>. He is the Great Soul, the <term>Para Brahma</term> <gloss>the Supreme Absolute or Ultimate Reality</gloss>, the creator and the destroyer of the universe. All of existence remains within Him in both manifest and unmanifest forms. He exists in the form of **Purusha** <gloss>the primordial Male Principle or Cosmic Being</gloss> and **Kala** <gloss>Time</gloss>. **Vishnu** is both the seen and the unseen; He is both the Being and the Time. Understand that His actions are like those of a child at play. He has no beginning, yet He is the Creator; He has no end, and is **Purusottama** <gloss>the most exalted of all beings</gloss>. From Him originate the unmanifest world and the individual soul. From Him also come intellect, the mind, and the <term>Tattwas</term> <gloss>the fundamental principles or building blocks of reality</gloss>: ether, air, fire, water, and earth. O **Rudra**, He is the <term>Golden Egg</term> <note>original: Hiranyagarbha; the cosmic seed or "womb of gold" from which the universe emerges</note>, and He is Himself His own final destination.

<meta>This page continues the discourse of the Lord Hari (Vishnu) speaking to Rudra (Shiva). Having established his divine roles in the previous page, Hari now details the specific mechanics of how the universe is created and the various categories of beings that inhabit it.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>GARUDA PURANAM.</header>

The Lord assumes a body for the purposes of creation. Having assumed the body of **Brahmā** <gloss>the creator god</gloss> with four mouths, and being pervaded by the quality of *Rajas* <note>original: Rajas; usually meaning passion or activity, though the text here equates it with the darkness of the initial creative labor</note>, he creates the entire world, both moveable and stationary. This entire universe, consisting of the celestials, **Asuras** <gloss>powerful beings often in conflict with the gods</gloss>, and human beings, lies inside the cosmic egg. As the Creator, he creates the universe; as **Vishnu**, he protects it; and he destroys it in the end. **Hari** <gloss>a name for Vishnu</gloss> himself is the destroyer. Having assumed the body of **Brahmā**, **Hari** creates the universe and as **Vishnu** he protects it. And assuming the form of **Rudra** <gloss>Shiva</gloss>, the Lord destroys the universe at the end of a **Kalpa** <term>Kalpa</term> <gloss>a vast eon or cycle of time in Hindu cosmology</gloss>. When **Brahmā** was engaged in the work of creation, He, assuming the form of a boar <note>referring to the Varaha avatar of Vishnu</note>, held up with his tusks the earth which had sunk under the water. Hear, O **Shankara** <note>a title for Shiva meaning "the cause of happiness"</note>, I will describe all in short, beginning with the first creation of the deity.

The first creation is *Mahat* <term>Mahat</term> <gloss>the "Great Principle" or the first stage of cosmic intelligence</gloss>; it is simply a metamorphosis of **Brahmā**. The second creation is that of *Tanmatras* <term>Tanmatras</term> <gloss>the subtle elementary particles or essences of the senses</gloss>. This is known as the creation of elements. The third is called *Vaikārika* <note>the evolutionary creation of the senses and the mind</note> or the creation of organs. This is the material <note>original: Prākrita</note> or elementary creation originating from the intellect. 

The fourth is the primary creation—and all the stationary bodies <note>such as plants and minerals</note> are known as primary creations. The next is the *Tiryyaksrotas* <margin>Literally: the stream of beings living according to their animal nature; i.e., beasts and animals</margin> who are otherwise called *Tiryyakjonya*. The sixth is the *Urdhasrotasat* <margin>Literally: the stream of beings tending upwards; refers to the creation of the celestial gods</margin> which is called the creation of the celestials. The seventh is the creation of *Arvakasrotas* <margin>Also called Arvaraskotasas; those who eat by swallowing down; refers to human beings</margin> or human beings. The eighth is the creation of *Anugrahas* <margin>A specific class of deities or mental dispositions</margin> pervaded by the qualities of *Sattwa* <gloss>goodness and purity</gloss> and *Tamas* <gloss>ignorance and inertia</gloss>.

<summary>Hari describes the process of cosmic creation, explaining how he manifests as Brahma to create, Vishnu to protect, and Rudra to destroy, while listing the eight distinct stages of universal evolution.</summary>

<keywords>Trimurti, Brahma, Vishnu, Rudra, creation cycles, Kalpa, Mahat, Tanmatras, evolution of beings, Gunas</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse from the previous section where Vishnu, in the form of the creator god Brahmā, began the process of bringing the universe into existence. Having described the first stages of cosmic intelligence and the subtle elements, the narrative now shifts to the specific origins of various beings and the cycle of day and night.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>

-># GARUDA PURANAM.<-

These are the five orders of the *Vaikarika* <gloss>organic or transformed</gloss> creation. There are three orders of the elementary and organic creation. *Koumāra* <gloss>the creation of the "ever-youthful" sages</gloss> makes the ninth. O **Rudra** <note>original: Rudra; a name for Shiva, the destroyer, who is listening to this account</note>, there are four orders of creation beginning with the celestials and ending with the stationary creation <gloss>such as plants and minerals</gloss>.

While engaged in the work of creation, **Brahmā** first procreated his mind-born sons. Then, desirous of creating the four classes of beings—namely the celestials, **Asuras** <gloss>powerful beings often at odds with the gods</gloss>, **Pitris** <gloss>the ancestral spirits</gloss>, and human beings, all going under the name of *Amva* <note>original: Amva; likely a reference to "Ambhas," meaning the waters or the collective "seed" of creation</note>—he adored his own self. <note>The passage is not clear. Perhaps by the expression "adored his own self" the author means that Brahmā was engaged in the process of mental retrospection.</note> As the lord of beings, although himself of unfettered soul, concentrated his soul, being desirous of creating, passion overspread him and first from forth his hips came out the **Asuras**. And then he renounced his person surcharged with *Tama* <gloss>the quality of darkness or inertia</gloss>; and his darkness, on being renounced by him, O **Shankara** <note>another title for Shiva</note>, was converted into Night. 

Having assumed another body, he became desirous of creating and felt delight. Then, O **Hara** <note>another name for Shiva</note>, came out from the mouth of **Brahmā** the celestials surcharged with the quality of *Sattwa* <gloss>the quality of goodness and purity</gloss>. On being renounced by him, his body, surcharged with the quality of *Sattwa*, was converted into Day. Therefore it is that the **Asuras** are powerful in the night and the celestials during the day. 

He then assumed a person fraught with the quality of goodness, and then sprang from him the ancestors <gloss>the Pitris</gloss>. And that body, on being renounced by him, became Twilight, remaining between day and night. Then having assumed a body fraught with the quality of *Rajas* <note>original: Rajas; usually passion or activity, though the text here identifies it with a form of darkness or striving</note>, he created human beings. And on being renounced by him, that form became moonlight which is termed *Praksandhya.* <note>Meaning going before twilight.</note> 

Moonlight, Night, Day and Twilight are his bodies. And then he assumed another body fraught with the quality of darkness and thereat sprung hunger—

<summary>This page describes the divine origin of different classes of beings—Asuras, gods, ancestors, and humans—and explains how the different times of day (Night, Day, Twilight, and Moonlight) are the cast-off bodies of the creator god Brahmā.</summary>
<keywords>Brahmā, Asuras, Pitris, Sattwa, Tama, Rajas, Hindu cosmogony, creation of day and night, Puranic mythology</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the creation of the universe by the god Brahmā. Having previously described the emergence of the primary elements and the initial orders of life, the text now details the specific origins of various supernatural beings, animals, and the structure of human society according to Vedic tradition.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>

-># THE GARUDA PURANA<-

From him <note>referring to Brahmā, the creator</note> and from hunger, wrath was born. **Brahmā** then created the **Rakshasas** <term>Rakshasas: a class of powerful, often fierce supernatural beings</term> who were tormented by hunger. Those among them who cried out, "Save him!" are known as the **Rakshas** <note>original: Rakshas; from the root 'raksha,' to protect</note>, while those who cried, "We shall eat him up!" are called **Yakshas** <term>Yakshas: nature spirits or guardians of wealth</term>, a name derived from *Yakshana* <gloss>the act of eating</gloss>. From the slithering movement (*Sarpana*) of his hair, the serpents were produced. In his anger, he generated beings of a naturally wrathful temperament. O sinless one, the **Gandharvas** <term>Gandharvas: celestial musicians and singers of the heavenly courts</term> then emerged while singing. All these various beings were brought into existence by him.

He created goats from his mouth and cattle from his belly and sides. From his feet, he brought forth horses, elephants, asses, and camels. From the hairs of his body, he produced medicinal herbs characterized by their fruits and roots. The fair-complexioned male sheep, horses, mules, and asses are categorized as *grāmya* <gloss>domesticated or household animals</gloss>.

Now listen as I describe the wild animals. These include beasts of prey, cloven-hoofed animals, elephants, monkeys, and—fifthly—birds, sixthly, aquatic creatures, and seventhly, reptiles. From his eastern and other various mouths, he created the **Rik** <note>The Rig Veda, the oldest of the four Vedas</note> and the other **Vedas** <term>Vedas: the primary holy scriptures of Hinduism</term>. 

The social orders of humanity also sprang from his body: the **Brahmanas** <gloss>the priestly and scholarly class</gloss> originated from his mouth, the **Kshatriyas** <gloss>the warrior and ruling class</gloss> from his arms, the **Vaishyas** <gloss>the merchants and agriculturalists</gloss> from his thighs, and the **Shudras** <gloss>the laboring and service class</gloss> from his feet. 

Specific spiritual realms were assigned to each: the region of **Brahma** is for the **Brahmanas**, the realm of **Shakra** <note>another name for Indra, the king of the gods</note> is for the **Kshatriyas**, the realm of **Marut** <note>the wind-god</note> is for the **Vaishyas**, and the realm of the **Gandharvas** is for the **Shudras**. Those who strictly follow the vows of **Brahmacharya** <term>Brahmacharya: a stage of life focused on celibate study and spiritual discipline</term> attain the region of **Brahma**. Householders who duly perform their duties go to the region of the Creator. Those living as hermits in the forest acquire the region of the seven **Rishis** <term>Rishis: ancient semi-divine sages</term>. Finally, the sphere of the **Yatis** <term>Yatis: wandering ascetics who have renounced all worldly ties</term>, who travel where they will, is the eternal region.

## CHAPTER V.

**HARI** <note>another name for the god Vishnu, the Preserver</note> said:—Having created the world and all <unclear>orders</unclear> of beings, the Lord created his mind-begotten sons <note>beings born directly from his thought rather than physical birth</note> for multiplying his creation; namely **Dharma**, **Rudra**, **Manu**, **Sanaka**, **Sanātana**, **Bhrigu**, **Sanatkumāra**, **Ruchí**, **Shuddha**, **Maríchi**, **Atri**, **Angira**, **Pulastya**, **Pulaha**, **Kratu**, **Nārada** and the **Pitris Varhisadas** <note>original: Patris; refers to a specific class of ancestral spirits or "fathers" associated with sacred fires</note>.

The **Lotus-sprung deity** <note>Brahma, the creator, who is often depicted sitting on a lotus emerging from Vishnu’s navel</note>, from his right thumb, created **Daksha** endued with four forms and his wife from his left thigh. **Daksha** begat on her beautiful daughters. He gave them away unto the sons of **Brahmā** and conferred **Sati** on **Rudra** <note>a fierce form of the god Shiva</note>. Numberless, highly powerful sons were born unto **Rudra**. He conferred the auspicious **Khyāti**, matchless in beauty, on **Bhrigu**. **Bhrigu** begat on her **Dhātā** and **Vidhātā**. He also begat **Shree** <term>Shree: also known as Lakshmi, the goddess of fortune and beauty</term> who was **Nārāyana’s** <note>Vishnu's</note> wife. **Hari** begat on her himself **Bala** and **Ukshmadi**. **Ayati** and **Niyati** were the two daughters of the high-souled **Manu**. They were the wives of **Dhātā** and **Vidhātā**. They gave birth to two sons, **Prāna** and **Mrikandu**. **Mārkandeya** <note>a famous sage known for his extreme longevity and devotion</note> was the son of **Mrikandu**.

**Marichi’s** wife **Sambuti** gave birth to **Pournamāsa**. **Viraja** and **Sarvaga** were the two sons of that high-souled one. **Angiras** begat on **Smriti** sons and daughters namely **Sinivalee**, **Kuhu**, **Rākā** and **Anumati**. **Atri** begat on **Anasuyā** sinless sons, **Soma**, **Durvāsā** and the yogin **Dattātreya**. **Pulastya** begat on **Priti** **Dottoli**. Three sons, **Karman**, **Arthavira** and **Sahishnu** were begotten by the patriarch **Pulaha** on his wife **Kshamā**. **Krati** begat on his wife **Sumati** the sixty thousand **Rishis** <term>Rishis: inspired poets or sages</term> of controlled passions by name **Vālakhilyas** <term>Vālakhilyas: a group of tiny, thumb-sized divine sages who follow the sun's chariot</term>. They were all of the height of a thumb and effulgent <gloss>shining brightly</gloss> like the burning sun. **Vashistha** begat on **Urjjā** seven sons, viz, **Rajo**, **Gatra**, **Urdhavāhu**, **Sharana**, **Anagha**, **Sutapa**, **Sukra**. These are the **seven Rishis** <note>the Saptarishi, or Seven Great Sages, who represent the stars of the Big Dipper and guide humanity</note>.

Daksha <note>one of the primary progenitors of mankind</note> conferred **Swāhā** <note>the goddess of the ritual call used when making offerings to fire</note> on the fire-god who had assumed a physical body. O Hara <note>an address to the god Shiva</note>, from him **Swāhā** obtained three highly radiant sons: **Pāvaka**, **Pavamāna**, and **Shuchi**, all of whom are "eaters of water" <note>a poetic reference to fire, particularly the heat that evaporates water or the lightning that originates in the clouds</note>. **Swadhā** <note>the ritual call used when making offerings to ancestors</note> gave birth to **Menā** and **Vaitarani**. They were both **Brahmavadinis** <note>women who interpret and discourse upon divine science and the ultimate reality</note>. **Menā** was married to **Himachala** <note>the personification of the Himalayan mountains</note>.

O Hara! the Lord formerly appointed **Manu Swayambhu** <note>the "Self-Created" first man and lawgiver</note>, who had sprung from the self of **Brahmā** and resembled him, to rule over all creatures. **Swayambhuva Manu** accepted as his wife **Shatarupā** <gloss>having a hundred forms</gloss> who had purified herself of all sins through intense spiritual penances. To that person **Satarupā** bore two sons, **Priyavrata** and **Uttānapada**, and daughters named **Prasuti**, **Akuti**, and **Devahuti**. Of them, **Manu** gave **Akuti** to the sage **Ruchi**, **Prasuti** to **Daksha**, and **Devahuti** to **Kardama**. To **Ruchi** were born **Yajna** <gloss>Sacrifice</gloss> and **Dakshinā** <gloss>Gifts or fees given in a sacrifice</gloss>. In turn, **Yajna** fathered twelve highly powerful sons with **Dakshinā**, known by the name **Yamas** <note>a specific class of minor deities or celestial beings</note>.

The most excellent **Daksha** fathered twenty-four daughters. They were **Sraddhā** <gloss>Veneration or Faith</gloss>, **Lakshmi** <gloss>The goddess of wealth and fortune</gloss>, **Dhriti** <gloss>Patience or Firmness</gloss>, **Tushti** <gloss>Satisfaction</gloss>, **Pushti** <gloss>Nourishment or Thriving</gloss>, **Medhā** <gloss>Intelligence</gloss>, **Kriyā** <gloss>Ritual action</gloss>, **Buddhi** <gloss>Intellect</gloss>, **Lajjā** <gloss>Bashfulness or Modesty</gloss>, **Vapu** <gloss>The Body</gloss>, **Sānti** <gloss>Success or Peace</gloss>, **Riddhi** <gloss>Fame or Prosperity</gloss>, and **Kriti** <gloss>Righteousness or Action</gloss>. These thirteen daughters of **Daksha** were wedded by **Dharma** <note>the personification of Duty and Universal Law</note> for the purpose of procreating sons. 

The remaining daughters were **Khyāti** <gloss>Renown</gloss>, **Sāti** <gloss>Chastity</gloss>, **Sambhuti** <gloss>Birth or Origin</gloss>, **Smriti** <gloss>Memory</gloss>, **Priti** <gloss>Gratification or Love</gloss>, **Kshamā** <gloss>Forgiveness</gloss>, **Saunati** <gloss>Reverence</gloss>, **Anasuya** <gloss>Good-natured or free from envy</gloss>, **Urjjā** <gloss>Energy</gloss>, **Swāhā**, and **Swadhā** <gloss>These names refer to the sacred words uttered while offering oblations to the sacrificial fire or the ancestors</gloss>. The foremost of the ascetics—**Bhrigu**, **Bhava**, **Marichi**, **Angira**, **Pulastya**, **Pulaha**, **Kratu**, **Atri**, **Vasistha**, and the **Pitris** <note>The Ancestral Spirits</note>—duly married all these daughters, beginning with **Khyāti**. Then **Sraddhā** <term>Faith</term> brought forth **Kāma** <term>Desire</term>,<gloss>sexual desire</gloss> **Chala** <term>Prosperity</term>,<gloss>The goddess Lakshmi</gloss> and **Darpa** <term>Pride</term>.<gloss>pride</gloss> She also bore **Niyama** <term>Observance</term><gloss>restraint</gloss> and **Dhriti** <term>Fortitude</term><gloss>nourishment</gloss> as her sons, as well as **Tushti** <term>Satisfaction</term> and **Santosha** <term>Contentment</term>,<gloss>contentment</gloss> and **Pushti** <term>Thriving</term> and **Lobha** <term>Greed</term>.<gloss>avarice</gloss> **Medhā** <term>Intellect</term><gloss>intellect</gloss> brought forth **Shruta** <term>Sacred Knowledge</term><gloss>knowledge or what is heard through tradition</gloss> and **Kriyā** <term>Action</term>,<gloss>action</gloss> **Dandam** <term>Punishment</term>,<gloss>punishment</gloss> **Laya** <term>Order</term><gloss>justice</gloss> and **Vinaya** <term>Discipline</term>,<gloss>humiliation</gloss> and **Buddhi** <term>Intelligence</term>,<gloss>intellect</gloss> **Bodh** <term>Understanding</term><gloss>apprehension</gloss> and **Lajjā** <term>Modesty</term>, **Vinaya** <term>Humility</term>, **Vapu** <term>Form</term><gloss>body</gloss> and **Vyavasaya** <term>Resolution</term><gloss>exertion</gloss> as her sons. And **Shanti** <term>Peace</term> brought forth **Kshemā** <term>Well-being</term> and **Riddhi** <term>Prosperity</term>, **Sukham** <term>Happiness</term><gloss>felicity</gloss> and **Kirti** <term>Fame</term> and **Yaças** <term>Glory</term>.<gloss>fame</gloss> These are the offspring of **Dharma** <note>The god of Righteousness and Cosmic Order</note>.

**Kāma's** <term>Desire</term> wife was **Rati** <term>Pleasure</term> whose son was **Harsha** <term>Joy</term>.<gloss>joy</gloss> **Daksha** <note>One of the lords of creation</note> once undertook the celebration of a horse sacrifice <note>The Ashvamedha, a grand Vedic ritual to assert imperial sovereignty</note> at which all his sons-in-law were invited. They all went there accompanied with their wives except **Rudra** <note>A fierce form of the god Shiva</note> and **Sati** <note>The daughter of Daksha and wife of Shiva</note>. But **Sati** went there uninvited and was insulted by **Daksha**. Having renounced her body <note>This refers to Sati's self-immolation in the sacrificial fire after her father insulted her husband</note>, she was again begotten on **Menā** by **Himavan** <note>The personification of the Himalayas</note>. **Gouri** <note>The "Golden One," another name for Parvati</note> became **Shambhu's** <note>A benevolent name for Shiva</note> wife, whose sons were **Vināyaka** <note>Ganesha, the remover of obstacles</note> and **Kumāra** <note>Skanda or Kartikeya, the god of war</note>. **Rudra**, the powerful master of **Bhringi** <note>A devoted attendant of Shiva</note>, was by nature wrathful. He destroyed **Daksha's** sacrifice and imprecated a curse on him, saying "You will be born as a man, in the family of **Dhruva**."

## CHAPTER VI.

---

**HARI** <note>The god Vishnu</note> said:—**Uttanāpada** begat a son on **Suruchi** by name **Uttama** and another on **Suniti** by name **Dhruva** <note>A famous devotee in Hindu mythology who became the Pole Star</note> who attained to the most exalted station, having adored **Janārddana** <note>Vishnu, "the mover of men"</note>, the god of gods, by the favour of the ascetic. **Dhruva's** son **Shnisthi** was greatly strong and highly powerful. And his son was the greatly intelligent **Prachīnavarhi**.

His son was **Divanjaya** whose son was **Ripu**. His son was known as the beautiful **Manu Chākshusha** <term>The Sixth Manu</term>.<gloss>the progenitor presiding over the sixth cosmic age</gloss> His son was **Ruru** whose son was the beautiful **Anga**. Anga's son was **Vena**, who was an atheist and an irreligious person. **Vena**, the perpetrator of iniquities, was killed by the **Rishis** <term>Sages</term> with **Kuçā** <term>Sacred Grass</term>.<gloss>desmostachya bipinnata, a sharp grass used in Vedic rituals</gloss> They then churned his thigh <note>A ritual process to extract a successor from a dead ruler's body</note> for a son and thereat sprang a son, greatly dwarfish and black. They then said "sit down, sit down" <note>original: "nishāda"</note> and he was called **Nishāda** <term>The Hunter</term>, dwelling on the mount **Vindhyā**. Then the twice-born ones <note>The Brahmins or high-caste priests</note> suddenly churned his right thigh. From there sprang a son assuming the mental form of **Vishnu** <term>The Preserver</term>, by name **Prithu**. By that son **Vena** repaired to the celestial region. <note>In Hindu tradition, a virtuous son can save his father from hell through his own merit.</note> The king milched the earth <note>A metaphor for Prithu organizing agriculture and resources</note> for giving life to his subjects.

**Prithu’s** son was **Antarddhāna** whose son again was **Havirdhāna**. His son **Prāchinavarhi** became the Lord Paramount of the world. He espoused the daughter of the ocean of salt water and begat on her ten **Prāchinavarhis** who were called **Prāchetas** and were masters of the science of archery. They all practised the same religious austerities and remained immersed in the bed of the deep <note>The ocean</note> for ten thousand years. They attained to the dignity of the **Patriarchs** <term>Prajapatis</term>;<gloss>progenitors of mankind</gloss> and their wife was **Marishā**. Of her was born **Daksha** by the imprecation <note>curse</note> of **Bhava** <term>Shiva</term>.

As before **Daksha** again created four classes of beings from his mind. Obstructed by **Hara** <term>Shiva</term> <gloss>another name for Shiva, meaning "the Remover"</gloss> they did not multiply. The Patriarch then desired to carry on the work of creation by sexual intercourse. He then espoused **Asikni**, the daughter of the Patriarch **Varuna** <term>God of the Waters</term>. He begat on the daughter of **Varuna** a thousand sons. The end of the earth being described by **Narada** <term>The Divine Sage</term> they went there to learn it and did not return. <note>Narada convinced them to pursue spiritual liberation rather than fathering children.</note> On their being lost **Daksha** created another thousand, named **Savalakha**, who also followed the foot-steps of their brothers, O **Hara**. Worked up with anger **Daksha** cursed **Nārada** saying "You will obtain a birth" and he was again born as the son of the ascetic **Kashyapa**. On his sacrifice

<page-num>18</page-num>
# GARUDA PURANAM.

After his sacrifice was destroyed, **Daksha** imprecated the terrific **Maheshwara** <term>The Great Lord Shiva</term>, saying "Having worshipped thee with necessary articles, the twice-born <note>The "twice-born" (dvija) refers to the upper three castes of ancient Indian society who undergo a spiritual initiation.</note> will leave them aside. Even in another birth, your hostilities will not terminate, O **Shankara** <gloss>The Giver of Happiness, a name for Shiva</gloss>."

**Daksha** begat on **Asikni** sixty beautiful daughters. He gave away two of them unto **Angiras**. He conferred two on **Krishāshwa**, ten on **Dharma** <gloss>The personification of Duty and Righteousness</gloss>, thirteen on **Kashyapa** <term>A progenitor sage</term>, and twenty-seven on **Indu** <term>The Moon god</term>. He conferred **Suprabhā** and **Bhāmini** on **Vahuputra**. And O **Mahadeva** <gloss>Great God</gloss>, he gave unto **Aristhanemi** his four daughters, namely **Manoramā**, **Bhānumati**, **Vishālā**, and **Vahudā**. He conferred on **Krishāshwa** the daughters **Supraja** and **Jayā**.

**Arundhati**, **Vasu**, **Yâmi**, **Lamvâ**, **Bhānu**, **Marutvati**, **Sangkalpa**, **Muhurta**, **Sādhyā**, and **Vishwā**—these ten are known as the wives of **Dharma**. I will now describe the names of the wives of **Kashyapa**. They were **Aditi**, **Diti**, **Danu**, **Kālā**, **Anāyu**, **Sinhikā**, **Muni**, **Kadru**, **Prādhā**, **Ira**, **Krodhā**, **Vinātā**, **Surabi**, and **Khagā**. **Vishwâ** gave birth to the **Vishwadevas** <gloss>The Universal Gods</gloss> and **Sadhya** to the **Sadhyas** <gloss>A class of celestial beings</gloss>; **Murutvati** to **Marudyant** and **Vasu** to the **Vasus** <term>The eight elemental deities</term>. From **Bhānu** were born the **Bhānus** <gloss>Suns or rays of light</gloss> and from **Muhurtta** the **Muhurttas** <note>Personifications of divisions of time, roughly 48 minutes each.</note>. **Ghosa** was born of **Lamva**, and **Nagavithi** <note>original: "The path of the serpent," often identified with the Milky Way</note> was born of **Yami** <gloss>Night</gloss>. All the objects of the world were born of **Arundhati**, and **Sankalpa** <term>Pious Determination</term> was the son of **Sankalpa**. **Apa**, **Dhruva**, **Soma**, **Dhava**, **Anila**, **Anala**, **Pratyusha**, and **Prabhasa** are the names of the eight **Vasus**.

**Apa’s** sons were **Vaitundya**, **Srama** <gloss>Weariness</gloss>, **Sranta** <gloss>Fatigue</gloss>, and **Dhani**. And the son of **Dhruva** <gloss>The Pole Star</gloss> was the great **Kala** <term>Time</term>, the cherisher of the world. The son of **Soma** <gloss>The Moon</gloss> was **Varchas** <gloss>Light</gloss>, by whom was generated **Varchaswi** <gloss>Radiance</gloss>. **Dhava** begat on his wife **Manoharâ** the sons **Rubina**, **Hutahavya**, **Shishira**, **Prana**, and **Ramana**. **Anila’s** <gloss>The Wind</gloss> wife was **Shivā**. Her sons were **Pulomya** and **Avijnatagati** <note>original: "Unknowable motion"</note>. These were the two sons of **Anila**. The son of **Agni** <gloss>Fire</gloss>, **Kumara** <term>The Divine Youth, Kartikeya</term>, was born in a clump of **Sara** reeds whose...
