The sons were **Shâkha**, **Vishâkha**, **Naigameya**, and **Prishtata**. The son of **Kirtikas** was known as **Kartikeya** <note>Also known as Skanda, the celestial general and god of war.</note>. **Pratyusha's** son was the ascetic **Davala**. **Vishwakarmâ**, the celebrated architect of the celestials, was the son of **Prabhâsa**. His sons were **Ajaikapada**, **Ahirvadhna**, **Twastri**, and **Rudra**, all very energetic. And the own begotten son of **Twastri** was the great ascetic **Vishwarupa**. There are eleven **Rudras** <gloss>Fierce forms of the god Shiva</gloss>, the lords of the three worlds. They are **Hara**, **Vahurupa**, **Tryamvaka**, **Aparajita**, **Vrishâkapi**, **Shambhu**, **Kapardi**, **Raivata**, **Mrigavyadha**, **Sarva**, and **Kapali**, O great ascetic. **Soma's** <gloss>The Moon god</gloss> wives were twenty-seven in number known as stars <note>These correspond to the Nakshatras, or lunar mansions, used in ancient Indian astronomy.</note>. **Kashyapa** begat on **Aditi** twelve suns. **Vishnu**, **Shakra**, **Aryama**, **Dhâtâ**, **Twastha**, **Pushâ**, **Vivashwan**, **Savita**, **Mitra**, **Varuna**, **Angshuman**, and **Bhaga**—these were the twelve <term>Adityas</term> <gloss>A group of solar deities representing different aspects of the sun and social order</gloss>.

**Hiranyakashipu** and **Hiranyakaksha** were born of **Diti**. A daughter, by name **Sinhika**, was also born who was married to **Viprachitti**. **Hiranyakashipu** had four very powerful sons, namely, **Anuhlāda**, **Hlāda**, the powerful **Pralhada**, and **Sanghlada**. Of them **Pralhada** was devoted to **Vishnu**. The sons of **Sanghlāda** were **Sivi**, **Ayushman**, and **Vashkala**. **Pralhada's** son was **Virochana** who begat **Bali**, who again had a hundred sons of whom **Vāna** was the oldest, O bull-emblemed deity <note>A reference to Lord Shiva, whose vehicle is the bull Nandi.</note>.

All the sons of **Hiranyākasha** were also gifted with great prowess—**Utkara**, **Shakoni**, **Bhutasantapana**, **Mahānābha**, **Mahāvāhu**, and **Kālanābha**.

**Danu's** sons were **Dwimurdhā**, **Shankara**, **Ayomukha**, **Shankusira**, **Kapila**, **Samvara**, **Ekachakra**, **Mahāvāhu**, the highly powerful **Taraka**, **Swarbhānu**, **Vrishaparva**, the great <term>Asura</term> <gloss>A class of powerful, often titanic beings who frequently contended with the gods</gloss> **Puloma**, and the mighty **Viprachitti**.

**Swarbhānu** had a daughter by name **Suprabha**, and **Sarmistha** was the daughter of **Vrishaparvan**, who had two other celebrated daughters, namely **Upadānavi** and **Hayasira**. **Vaishwānara** had two daughters named **Pulomā** and **Kālakā**, who were both married to **Māricha**. ...sixty thousand sons, the leading **Dānavas** <gloss>A race of powerful beings often in conflict with the gods</gloss>. The **Poulamas** and **Kāla Kāñjas** are known as the sons of **Māricha**.

**Viprachitti** fathered on the lineage of **Sinhikā** several powerful sons: **Shalya** the strong, **Nābha** the powerful, **Vātāpi**, **Namuchi**, **Itwala**, **Khasrimā**, **Anjaka**, **Naraka**, and **Kālanābha**.

In the family of the **Daitya** <gloss>A class of giants or titans</gloss> **Prahlāda**, the **Nivātakavachas** <note>Literally "those with impenetrable armor," a class of demons who lived deep in the ocean.</note> were born. Six daughters, gifted with great energy, were born to **Tāmrā**, named **Shuki**, **Syeni**, **Bhāsi**, **Sugrivi**, **Suchi**, and **Gridhrika**. **Shuki** gave birth to parrots, owls, and crows; **Syeni** to hawks; **Bhāsi** to kites; **Gridhrika** to vultures; **Suchi** to water-fowl; and **Sugrivi** to horses, camels, and asses. These are known as the offspring of **Tāmrā**.

**Vinatā** gave birth to two sons celebrated as **Garuda** <note>The divine king of birds and the mount of Lord Vishnu.</note> and **Aruna** <note>The charioteer of the sun god and personification of the dawn.</note>. The offspring of **Surasā** were a thousand powerful serpents. **Kadrū** also had a thousand sons—many-hooded serpents of unmitigated prowess. The most celebrated amongst them were **Shesha** <note>The great serpent who supports the world on his hoods.</note>, **Vāsuki**, **Takshaka**, **Shānka**, **Sweta**, **Mahāpadma**, **Kumvala**, **Ashwatara**, **Elapatra**, **Nāga**, **Karkota**, **Dhananjaya**, and many other deadly and poisonous serpents.

**Krodhā** gave birth to highly powerful **Pishāchas** <gloss>Flesh-eating spirits or ghosts</gloss>. **Surabhi** gave birth to kine <note>An archaic term for cows.</note> and buffaloes. **Irā** gave birth to trees, creepers, grass, and other vegetation. **Khagā** gave birth to **Yakshas** <gloss>Nature spirits and guardians of earthly treasures</gloss> and **Rakshas** <gloss>Shape-shifting spirits or demons</gloss>, and **Muni** gave birth to **Apsaras** <gloss>Celestial nymphs and dancers of the gods</gloss>. **Aristha** gave birth to the highly powerful **Gandharvas** <gloss>Celestial musicians and singers</gloss>.

Then were born the deities known as **Maruts**, forty-nine in number <note>The Maruts are storm deities and companions of Indra.</note>, namely: **Ekajyoti**, **Duryyoti**, **Trijyoti**, **Chaturjyoti**, **Ekashukra**, **Dwishukra**, the highly powerful **Trishukra**, **Idrik**, **Anyadrik**, **Sadrik**, **Pratisadrik**, **Mita**, **Samita**, the highly powerful **Sumita**, **Ritajit**, **Satyajit**, **Sushena**, **Senajit**, **Atimitra**, **Amitra**, **Duramitra**, **Ajila**, **Rita**, **Ritadharmā**, **Viharta**, **Varuna**, **Dhruva**, **Vidhārana**, **Grihamekagana**, **Idriksha**, **Sadriksha**, **Etadriksha**, **Mita**, **Shana**, **Etana**, **Prasadnksha**, **Sutra**, the great ascetic **Tadngugra**, **Dhvannibhasa**, **Vimukta**, **Vikshipa**, **Dyuti**, **Vasu**, **Valādrishya**, **Lābha**, **Kama**, **Jayi**, **Virat**, **Udveshana**, and **Guna**.

### CHAPTER VII.

---

**RUDRA SAID:—** I will describe in brief the adoration of **Suryya** <note>The Sun god, also spelled Surya</note> as instituted by **Shywambhuva** <note>Likely referring to Svayambhuva Manu, the first of the fourteen progenitors of humanity</note>, which is essential and gives enjoyment and emancipation <gloss>spiritual liberation from the cycle of rebirth</gloss>. Listen to it, O **Vyāsa** <note>The sage credited with compiling the Puranas and the Vedas</note>.

**RUDRA SAID:—** I will describe the adoration of the sun yielding religion, desire, and so forth.

**Om** <note>The primordial sacred sound of the universe</note>, salutation unto the seat of the sun. **Om**, salutation unto the form of the sun. **Om, Hrám, Hrim** <note>Sacred "seed-syllables" or Bija Mantras used to invoke specific energies</note>, salutation unto the sun. **Om** salutation unto the moon. **Om** salutation unto Mars. **Om** salutation unto Mercury. **Om** salutation unto Jupiter. **Om** salutation unto Venus. **Om** salutation unto Saturn. **Om** salutation unto **Ráhu** <note>The celestial body representing the north node of the moon</note>. **Om** salutation unto **Ketu** <note>The celestial body representing the south node of the moon</note>. **Om** salutation unto the bundle of effulgence.

O bull-emblemed deity <note>A reference to Lord Shiva, whose mount is the bull Nandi</note>, one should adore the sun and other planets by offering them seats, conveyances, water for washing feet, <term>Arghya</term> <gloss>A respectful ritual offering of water, flowers, or grain</gloss>, water for rinsing the mouth, water for bathing, raiments <gloss>clothing</gloss>, sacrificial threads, scents, flowers, incense, lamps, and salutation, by circumambulating them <gloss>walking around the deity in a clockwise circle</gloss> and performing the ceremony of <term>Visarjjana</term> <gloss>The formal ritual of bidding farewell to the deity at the end of the worship</gloss>.

**Om, Hám**, salutation unto the seat of **Shiva**. **Om, Hám**, salutation unto the form of **Shiva**. **Om, Hám** salutation unto his heart. **Om, Him**, unto the head, **Swáhá** <note>A ritual exclamation used when offering oblations into the sacred fire</note>. **Om, hum**, unto the tuft of hair, **Vashat** <note>A sacred ritual cry used to conclude a sacrificial chant</note>. **Om, Haim**, unto the amulet, **Hum**. **Om hum** unto the three eyes **Voushat** <note>An exclamation used to invoke divine energy or power</note>. **Om, Ham**, salutation unto the weapons. **Om, Ham**, salutation unto the immediate—

Faith, Prosperity, Art, Intelligence, Contentment, Radiance, and Intellect—these energies of **Saraswati** <gloss>the goddess of knowledge, music, and the arts</gloss> should be adored with sacred chants <gloss>mantrams</gloss> beginning with **Om** <note>the primordial sacred sound</note> and ending with **Namas** <gloss>salutation or "I bow to you"</gloss>.

## CHAPTER VIII.

---

**HARI** <note>a name for Lord Vishnu, the Preserver</note> said:—Having bathed in the **Mandapa** <term>Mandapa: a ritual pavilion or pillared hall built for sacred ceremonies</term> built on earth, one should adore **Vishnu** in a **Mandala** <term>Mandala: a symbolic geometric diagram used for meditation and ritual</term>.

With powders of five colors, this circular figure, known as **Vajranabha** <note>literally "Diamond-Navel," referring to a specific complex geometric pattern</note>, should be drawn. O **Rudra** <note>a name for Lord Shiva</note>, sixteen rooms should be drawn there. In the fourth and fifth corners, ritual strings should be placed. The strings in the corner should be extended to both the other corners. A man, well-versed in all these rites, should thus place strings in all the corners. Similarly, he should deal with the interior corners. The first should be placed in the center and then at the junction of the lines in the middle. In all the interior parts there are eight centers. The centers of the eastern and central figures should be connected with a string. O **Hara** <note>another name for Shiva, meaning "the Remover"</note>, the foremost of the twice-born <note>those initiated into spiritual life</note> should draw the base in the interior parts. O **Shiva**, connected with it, the pericarps <term>pericarp: the central seed-vessel of a flower, here representing the center of a lotus design</term> of the central string should be drawn. On two sides of the pericarps, an expert should draw the filaments <note>the delicate inner stalks of a flower</note>. A learned man should draw petals on their heads. O thou of firm vows, a worshipper, conversant with the

<summary>This page outlines the invocation of the seven energies of Saraswati and provides precise geometrical instructions for constructing the Vajranabha Mandala, a sacred diagram used in the worship of Vishnu.</summary>
<keywords>Saraswati, Mandala construction, Vajranabha, Vishnu ritual, sacred geometry, goddess energies, ritual architecture</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions for constructing the Vajranabha Mandala described on the previous page, detailing the specific colors for the lotus patterns and the ritual process of Nyasa—infusing the body with divine presence—to achieve union with Vishnu.</meta>

<page-num>24</page-num>

<header>GARUDA PURANAM.</header>

...knowledge of the true object; one should draw the shapes of lotuses <note>the lotus is a central symbol of purity and divine unfolding in Indian traditions</note> in all the centers.

Based on the division of the first ritual string, one should draw the gateways. With half that measure, one should create decorations for them. The pericarp <gloss>the seed-vessel at the center of the lotus</gloss> should be colored yellow, the filaments <gloss>the thread-like structures around the center</gloss> dark blue, the interior violet, and the petals crimson. The four sections should be filled with black powder, the doorways with white powder, and the five lines of the **Mandala** <term>Mandala: a sacred geometric diagram used to represent the universe or a divine residence</term> should be drawn in sequence with crimson, yellow, and dark blue. 

Having performed **Nyasa** <term>Nyasa: a ritual practice where the worshiper touches various parts of their body to "place" or "assign" the energy of specific deities or mantras onto themselves</term>, the practitioner should adore **Hari** <note>a name for Lord Vishnu, meaning "the one who removes sorrow"</note> within the five circles. He should assign his heart to **Vishnu**, the middle part of his body to **Sankarshana**, his head to **Pradyumna**, and the tuft of hair on his head to **Aniruddha** <note>Sankarshana, Pradyumna, and Aniruddha are the primary emanations or "Vyuha" forms of Vishnu representing different cosmic functions</note>. His entire body should be assigned to **Brahma** and his fingers to **Shridhara**. 

Meditating on the thought, “I am Vishnu,” he should perform the **Nyasa** of **Hari** in the lotus centers. He should perform the **Nyasa** of **Sankarshana** in the east, that of **Pradyumna** in the south, that of **Aniruddha** in the west, and that of **Brahma** in the north. He should then place **Shridhara**, **Indra** <note>the king of the gods</note>, and other deities in the corners associated with **Rudra** <note>a fierce form of Shiva</note> and others. Having honored them with scents and other ritual items, the worshiper attains the supreme station.*

->—:0:—<-

## CHAPTER IX.

---

**HARI** said:—Being initiated at the proper time and having his eyes covered with a cloth, a disciple should offer one hundred and eight oblations <note>ritual offerings, usually of clarified butter or grains, cast into a sacred fire</note> while reciting the principal **mantram** <term>mantram: a sacred sound, syllable, or phrase used in rituals to focus the mind or invoke a deity</term>.

---

* This chapter describes the drawing of a figure using various colors. Such a figure is essential for the performance of a religious rite. Even in modern Hindu marriages, such diagrams are drawn for the solemnization of religious ceremonies.

<page-num>25</page-num>

### GARUDA PURANAM.

O **Rudra** <note>a name for the god Shiva, who is being addressed here</note>, the number of offerings should be doubled for an initiation intended for those seeking a son <note>original: "Putraka-Homa"</note>, tripled for a spiritual practitioner <note>original: "Sadhaka"</note>, and quadrupled for a master leading others toward final liberation <note>original: "Nirvànadeshika"</note>. A person who destroys a teacher, an image of **Vishnu**, a **Brahmana** <note>a member of the priestly or scholarly class</note>, or a woman deserves death at the hands of those who are not initiated. I will now describe his **initiation** <note>original: "Dikshá"</note>, a rite capable of dissolving both worldly virtue and sin.

Having caused his disciples to sit outside, the teacher should help them concentrate their minds. O **Rudra**, he should consider them purified by the wind, transformed by the fire, and then sprinkled with sacred water. By uniting their inner consciousness with the ritual fire, he should offer it into the flames. He should meditate on the **sacred syllable Om** <term>Pranava: the primordial sound and instrument of all creation</term> within the sky and within the body. Next, he should unite this sound with the **knower of the field** <term>Kshetrajna: the divine spark or soul residing within the physical body</term>, as it is the true cause of the body's existence. Then, by imagining all the **feminine energies** <note>original: "Saktis"</note> of the deity within the various sacred diagrams <note>original: "Mandala"</note>, he should adore **Hari** <note>a name for Lord Vishnu</note>. 

There should be four gateways, following the order of the **shrine of Brahma** <note>original: "Brahma Tirtha"</note> and others. In this meditation, the disciple's head is the lotus; the fingers are its petals; the palm is the seed-vessel; and the nails are the filaments. Then, meditating there on **Hari**, the sun, and fire, the teacher—with a disciplined mind—should place his hand on the head of the disciple. For within the hand resides **Vishnu** himself; the teacher’s hand is considered the very hand of the deity, and through its touch, the entire collection of the disciple's sins is dissolved.

Having honored the disciple, covered his eyes with a piece of cloth, and placed him in front of the deity, the teacher should throw flowers there. He should cast the flowers toward the head of the deity, who holds the **Sarnga bow** <note>original: "Shrānga"; the celestial bow of Lord Vishnu</note>. He should speak the disciple's name as well as those of his wives. In the case of a **Shudra** <note>the class of people traditionally responsible for service and labor</note>, a skilled teacher should instead mention the name of the disciple's master.

<summary>This page details the ritual of initiation (Diksha) and fire offerings, emphasizing the teacher's role in purifying the disciple and acting as a vessel for Vishnu's divine presence.</summary>

<keywords>initiation, Diksha, Vishnu, fire offering, Homa, spiritual teacher, purification, meditation, sacred syllables, ritual hand gestures</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the ritual instructions from page 25, moving from general initiation rites conducted by a teacher to specific rituals for adoring Lakshmi and Saraswati, and beginning the discourse on the nine divine emanations.</meta>

# CHAPTER X.
---

**Hari** <note>a name for Lord Vishnu, who is the narrator here</note> said:—I will describe the adoration of **Shree** <note>another name for Lakshmi, the goddess of wealth and prosperity</note> and other deities within the **sacred sacrificial grounds** <term>Sthandila: a leveled and purified patch of earth prepared for a ritual offering</term> for the attainment of **spiritual perfections**. <term>Siddhi: supernatural powers or the successful completion of a religious rite</term>

**Om, Shrim**, salutation unto the great **Lakshmi**.

**Shram, Shrim, Shrum, Shroum, Shras** <note>these are "seed syllables" or Bija Mantras, used to invoke specific spiritual energies</note>. The practitioner should gradually worship the heart, the head, the **tuft of hair** <note>original: "shikha," the lock of hair at the crown of the head</note>, the **protective armor** <note>original: "kavacha," a mental or physical shield</note>, the eyes, the ritual seat, and the divine image. One who wishes to attain his desired objects should offer oblations to the **sacred diagram** <term>Mandala: a symbolic geometric map of the cosmos</term>, the sun, the moon, **Lakshmi** and her various forms in one corner, **Durga** <note>the warrior goddess</note>, the **Gana** <note>the divine attendants of the gods</note>, the **teacher** <note>original: "Guru"</note>, and the **protector of the region** <note>original: "Kshetrapala," a deity who guards the sacred site</note>—specifically **Vishnu**—within the **sacred diagram** which has the figure of a lotus drawn inside it, four painted doors, and sixty-four corners.

With the **sacred chant** <term>Mantram: a powerful sound or phrase used in prayer and meditation</term>, “**Om, Gham, tam, dham, Ham**, salutation unto the great **Lakshmi**,” he should adore **Lakshmi** together with all the members of her divine family as narrated before.

**Om, Soum**, salutation unto **Saraswati** <note>the goddess of knowledge, music, and the arts</note>. **Om, Hrim, Soun**, salutation unto **Saraswati**. **Om, Hrim**, speak, speak. O goddess of speech, **Swaha** <note>an exclamation used when casting offerings into the ritual fire</note>. **Om, Hrim**, salutation unto **Saraswati**.

# CHAPTER XI.
---

I will now describe the mode of adorning the **nine emanations** <term>Vyuhas: the various forms or manifestations of the supreme deity</term> as narrated unto the sage **Kashyapa** <note>one of the ancient and most revered Vedic sages</note>. Having drawn up the—

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for ritual purification and deity invocation. Having previously established the sacrificial grounds and the basic mantras for Lakshmi, the text now moves into the internal yogic process of purifying the body through the elements and the "Nyasa" ritual, where the practitioner identifies their own body with the divine form of Vishnu.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>
<header>GARUDA PURANAM.</header>

The practitioner should draw the **vital breath** <note>original: "prana"</note> through the head and place it in the sky through the navel. Then, using the **sacred syllable** <term>Mantram: a powerful sound or phrase used in prayer and meditation</term> **Ram** <note>the seed syllable of the fire element</note>, he should consume the body, identifying it with the rising vital air. He should then dissolve all things using the syllable **Yam** <note>the seed syllable of the air element</note>. With the syllable **Lam** <note>the seed syllable of the earth element</note>, he should flood the entire world, both the mobile and the immobile. Then, with the syllable **Vam** <note>the seed syllable of the water element</note>, he should meditate on the **celestial nectar** <note>original: "amrita," the drink of immortality</note>. 

Thereupon, by **meditation** <term>Dhyana: the practice of profound spiritual concentration</term>, he should visualize the four-armed deity <note>Vishnu</note> clad in yellow raiment, standing in the middle of a shining bubble, and imagine the deity's ritual bathing. He should next perform the three-fold **ritual of assignment** <term>Mantra-nyasa: the practice of mentally "placing" sacred syllables onto specific parts of the body to sanctify the practitioner and transform them into a fit vessel for the deity</term> upon the fingers and the body.

After the recitation of this prayer, using one composed of **twelve letters** <note>likely the fundamental mantra: "Om Namo Bhagavate Vasudevaya"</note>, he should make the assignment of the six limbs so that **Hari** <note>a name for Lord Vishnu, meaning "The Remover of Sorrow"</note> himself might appear there. Beginning with the thumb of the right hand, he should assign the middle finger to the "petal" <note>referring to the lotus of the heart</note>. Having assigned the two **seed syllables** <term>Vijas: "seed" sounds that represent the essence of a divine power</term> in the middle, he should assign them to the other limbs: namely, the heart, the head, the **crown of the head** <note>original: "shikha," the sacred lock of hair</note>, the mouth, the eyes, the belly, the back, the arms, the hands, the thighs, and the feet. 

Having formed the hand into the shape of a lotus, he should place the thumb in the middle. And there he should meditate on the Lord of all, the great undecaying Principle. Then, in the proper order, he should assign all the other sacred syllables to the forefingers and other fingers. He should then in order make assignment of the head, eyes, mouth, throat, heart, navel, buttocks, thighs, and feet. Having assigned the syllables of the six limbs or parts to the palms, he should assign others to the body. He should assign the five syllables to the five fingers, beginning with the thumb and ending with the youngest <note>the little finger</note>.

He should assign the syllable of the eye to the hand and then make assignment of the other limbs. Having assigned the heart-syllable to the heart, he should assign the head-syllable to the head, the **tuft of hair** <note>original: "shikha"</note> to the crown, and the **protective amulet** <note>original: "kavacha," a mental armor or shield created through prayer</note> to the entire body.

<summary>This page details the yogic process of purifying the practitioner's body using elemental seed syllables (Ram, Yam, Lam, Vam) and the complex "Nyasa" ritual, which involves mentally mapping the presence of Vishnu onto the practitioner's own limbs and fingers.</summary>
<keywords>Pranayama, Bija Mantras, Nyasa, Vishnu, purification, ritual meditation, twelve-letter mantra, Shikha, spiritual anatomy</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for the internal ritual of "Nyasa," where the worshipper identifies their body with the divine. Having previously purified the elements (earth, air, fire, water) within themselves, the practitioner now begins to construct the "Yoga-seat" within the heart to invite the presence of the deity.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>
<header>GARUDA PURANAM.</header>

Eyes should be assigned to the eyes and the weapon to the two hands. Having bound all the quarters <note>the cardinal and ordinal directions</note> with the weapon <note>the protective "Astra" mantra</note>, he should commence the rite of adoration. First of all, with a controlled mind, he should meditate on the **Seat of Yoga** <term>Yogapitha: a visualized spiritual throne within the heart</term> in his heart, and in due order on religion, knowledge, disassociation from the world, and prosperity. He should assign iniquity and other vices to the north-east and east <note>symbolically casting away negative traits from the sacred space</note>. The body, free of all these sins, should be then converted into a **Seat** <term>Pitha</term>. He should afterwards assign **The Infinite** <note>original: "Ananta," the celestial serpent who serves as the foundation of the universe</note>. Having meditated first, by means of the **Sacred Scriptures** <term>Vedas</term> and other texts on learning, he should meditate on the eight quarters as if they were the eight petals of a lotus born in a tank—a white lotus of a hundred petals covered with filaments. He should meditate on the **Sacred Circles** <term>Mandalas</term> that are identical with the sun, moon, and fire, and then think of the deity on each, one above the other. He should then perform the assignment of the eight energies of **The One with Beautiful Hair** <note>original: "Keshava," a name for Vishnu</note> stationed in the east, and of the ninth energy in the pericarp <note>the center of the lotus</note>.

Having thus meditated on and adored the **Seat of Yoga**, he should invoke its lord, **The Remover of Sorrow** <note>original: "Hari," a common name for Vishnu</note>, the holder of the **Sharnga bow** <note>the celestial bow of Vishnu</note>, and assign him there. He should assign the heart and other organs to the lotuses of the four quarters (east, etc.), the eye in the middle, and the weapon in the corners. He should assign the **sacred syllables** <term>Mantrams</term> of **The Collector** <note>original: "Sangkarshana," the first manifestation of the deity</note> to the east and other quarters, and **Vinata’s son** <note>Garuda, the divine eagle and mount of Vishnu</note> to the eastern and western doors, and the **Auspicious Disc** <note>original: "Sudarshana," Vishnu’s spinning sun-like weapon</note> of a thousand rays to the southern gate. He should assign **Fortune** <note>original: "Shree"</note> to the south, **Prosperity** <note>original: "Lakshmi"</note> to the north, the club to the northern gate, and the conch-shell to the corners.

An intelligent worshipper should place the **Sharnga bow** either on the right or on the left of the deity. Similarly, the conch-shell and disc should be placed on both sides. According to the distinction of their respective quarters, all the guardian deities of those directions should be assigned. In the same way, the various weapons, such as the thunderbolt and others, should be assigned. He should meditate on **The Creator** <note>original: "Brahma"</note> upwards and—

<summary>This page describes the construction of a spiritual "throne" (Yogapitha) within the heart through meditation, where the practitioner maps out the cardinal directions, various forms of Vishnu, and his divine weapons and consorts.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the sequence of ritual worship (puja) from the previous page. Having established the internal "Seat of Yoga" and visualized the sacred lotus within the heart, the practitioner now moves from mental visualization to physical hand gestures and the recitation of sacred sounds to invoke the various forms and weapons of Vishnu.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>
<header>GARUDA PURANAM.</header>

...from the serpent **The Infinite** <note>original: "Ananta"</note> downwards. Having meditated on and adored them all, the practitioner should display the **ritual hand gestures** <term>Mudra: symbolic or ritual gestures in Hinduism and Buddhism, used to channel energy and communicate with the divine</term>.

The **gesture of reverence** <term>Anjali: the common prayer position with palms folded together</term> is the first gesture, which quickly secures the favor of the deity. The **homage gesture** <term>Vandani</term> is the next; it is performed by placing the hands on the breast with the right hand raised halfway up. The **raised-thumb gesture** <term>Urdhangustha</term> is formed by clasping the fist of the left hand with the thumb of the right hand. In this position, the thumb of the left hand should be raised upward. These are the three ordinary forms of gestures, arranged according to the different forms of the idols being worshipped.

By intertwining the pinky fingers, eight further gestures are formed in sequence. These should be performed while reciting the first eight **sacred chants** <term>mantrams</term>. Using the thumb, the three successive fingers (ending with the pinky) should be bent. Then both hands should be bent. This is the gesture of **The Man-Lion** <note>original: "Narasimha," the fourth incarnation of Vishnu</note>. Having raised the left hand, the practitioner should whirl it; this is the gesture approved for **The Boar** <note>original: "Varâha," the incarnation who lifted the Earth from the cosmic ocean</note>. Having raised both fists, one should straighten the fingers of one hand and then bend them all; this gesture is called the **Limb** <term>Anga</term>. By intertwining the two fists in a specific order, the gestures for the ten guardian deities of the directions should be formed. 

The first vowel, the second vowel, the penultimate vowel, and the final vowel of the alphabet should be assigned, in that order, to the four primary emanations of the Lord: **The All-Pervasive** <note>Vasudeva</note>, **The Strong** <note>Bala</note>, **The Desired** <note>Kama</note>, and **The Unstoppable** <note>Aniruddha</note>. The sounds "Om, Tatsat, Hum, Kshroum, Bhus" are the sacred syllables for **The Settler of Men** <note>Narayana</note>, the Creator <note>Brahma</note>, the Preserver <note>Vishnu</note>, the Man-Lion, and the Boar forms. White, red, green, violet, blue, dark blue, crimson, cloud-colored, fire-colored, honey-colored, and tawny are the nine associated colors <note>The text lists eleven colors but refers to them as the "nine names," likely referring to nine specific groupings in the ritual</note>.

The seed-syllables *Kan, tan, jam, pam, Sham* invoke **The Winged One** <term>Garutman: another name for Garuda, the divine eagle and mount of Vishnu</term>.

*Jam, Kham, ban* invoke the **Beautiful Vision** <term>Sudarshana: the celestial spinning disc or weapon of Vishnu</term>. *Kham, cham, pham, Sham* invoke the **Club**.

*Vam, lam, man, Ksham* invoke the **Conch-shell**. *Gham, dham, Vam, bham, Ham* invoke **Prosperity** <note>original: "Shree," the goddess Lakshmi</note>. *Gam, jam, dam, vam, Sham* invoke **Nourishment** <note>original: "Pushti," the goddess of vitality and growth</note>.

**Dham, Vam**, are for the **garland of forest flowers** <note>original: "Vanamala," the characteristic flower garland worn by Vishnu</note>. **Dam, Sam**, are for the **mystic mark** <term>Srivatsa: an auspicious curl of hair or mark on the chest of Vishnu, signifying his greatness</term>.

**Chham, dam, pam, Yam** are for the **jewel** <term>Kaustubha: the celebrated "treasure of the ocean," a divine ruby worn by Vishnu on his chest</term>.

The practitioner should then say, "**I am the Infinite**" <note>original: "Ananta"</note>. These are the ten limbs of the Lord of gods.

**Garuda** <note>the divine eagle-man and vehicle of Vishnu</note> is the color of smoke. The **club** is white. **Nourishment** <note>original: "Pushti," a personified goddess of prosperity</note> is the color of a **Sirish flower** <note>a pale, yellowish-green blossom</note>. **Lakshmi** <note>the goddess of wealth and beauty</note> is gold-hued. The **conch-shell** is as brilliant as the full moon. The **mystic mark** <note>Srivatsa</note> is the color of a **Kunda flower** <note>a white jasmine</note>. The garland is composed of five colors. **The Infinite** <note>Ananta</note> is the color of a cloud. All the weapons described before have the brilliance of lightning.

Following the wisdom of the lotus-eyed deity, one should offer **respectful offerings** <term>Arghya: a formal offering of water, rice, and flowers</term>, **water for washing the feet** <term>Padya</term>, and other honors.

->## CHAPTER XII<-

---

**HARI** <note>a name for Vishnu, meaning "The Remover of Sorrows"</note> said:—I will now describe the order of adoration for achieving spiritual success.

The recollection of the **Great Soul** should be performed with the **sacred chants** <term>mantrams</term>, such as “**Om, Salutation** <note>original: "Om Namah..."</note>, etc.”

With the chant “**Yam, Vam, Lam, Ram**” <note>these syllables represent the elements of Air, Water, Earth, and Fire</note> the **purification of the body** should be performed.

With the chant “**Om, salutation**,” the mental image of the **four-armed deity** should be constructed.

Then, the three methods of creating idols should be performed. This is followed by the adoration of the **Seat of Yoga** <term>Yoga-pitha: a spiritual or physical pedestal upon which the deity is invited to sit</term> stationed within the heart.

**Om**, salutation unto **The Infinite** <note>Ananta</note>. **Om**, salutation unto **Virtue** <note>Dharma</note>. **Om**, salutation unto **Dispassion** <note>Vairagya</note>. **Om**, salutation unto **Knowledge** <note>Jnana</note>. **Om**, salutation unto **Sovereignty** <note>Aisvarya</note>. **Om**, salutation unto **Unrighteousness** <note>original: "Adharma" or "sin." In these rituals, the four opposites of the virtues are sometimes ritually acknowledged as the corners of the pedestal to be transcended or balanced</note>.

### GARUDA PURANAM.

*Lam*, salutation unto **Ananta** <note>original: "Ananta," the infinite serpent who supports the foundations of the world</note>, the lord of the nether region. *Kham*, salutation unto **Brahma**, the lord of all the worlds. **Om**, salutation unto the Lord **Vāsudeva** <note>a name for Vishnu meaning "The All-Pervading Lord"</note>.

**Om, Om,** Salutations; **Om, Nam,** Salutations; **Om, Mom,** Salutations; **Om, Bham,** Salutations; **Om, Gam,** Salutations; **Om, Vam,** Salutations; **Om, Tem,** Salutations; **Om, Vam,** Salutations; **Om, Sum,** Salutations; **Om, Dem,** Salutations; **Om, Vam,** Salutations; **Om, Yam,** Salutations.

**Om, Om,** Salutations; **Om, Nam,** Salutations; **Om, Mom,** Salutations; **Om, Nam,** Salutations; **Om, Ram,** Salutations; **Om, Yam,** Salutations; **Om, Nam,** Salutations; **Om, Yam,** Salutations; **Om**, salutation unto **Narayana** <note>Vishnu as the "Refuge of Men" or the one who dwells in the causal waters</note>. **Om**, salutation unto **Purusottama** <note>The "Supreme Person" or highest being</note>.

Salutation unto thee, O thou having lotus eyes; salutation unto thee O creator of the universe, O **Subramanya** <note>literally "dear to the priests," a title often associated with the deity Kartikeya, but here used as an epithet for the Divine</note>; salutation unto thee, O great **Purusha** <term>The cosmic, primordial man or consciousness</term>, O thou the first-born.

In **fire-sacrifice** <term>Homa: a ritual where offerings are made into a consecrated fire</term> rites, this mantra ending with the word "**hail**" <term>Swaha: the sacred exclamation used when casting offerings into the fire</term> should be recited. Having duly recited this mantra one hundred and eight times, the worshipper should offer **ritual water** <term>Arghya: an offering of water, flowers, and rice to welcome a deity</term> and bow unto the Deity again and again. Afterwards, having worshipped the fire, he should duly adore with his own mantra **Achyuta** <note>A name for Vishnu meaning "The Infallible" or "He who never falls"</note>, the god of gods.

Having first lighted the fire, fed it, and whirled it, one conversant with mantras should adore it in the **altar-pit** <term>Kunda: the specially constructed pit for the sacred fire</term> with auspicious results. Having first meditated on all, he should assign his mind to the **sacred circle** <term>Mandala</term>. Then with the **essential principle** <term>Tattwa</term> called Vāsudeva, he should offer one hundred and eight oblations. Then with **Sangkarshana** <note>The first manifestation of Vishnu, associated with cosmic strength</note> and other mantras, he should offer six adorations, three each time to his limbs as well as to the guardian deities of the quarters. Then in the end he should offer the **final oblation** <term>Purnāhuti: the "complete offering" that concludes the ceremony</term>. Then he should immerse his own self in the great principle which is beyond the range of speech. Then taking his seat and making again **sacred hand-gestures** <term>Mudras</term>, he should again bow. This is the daily fire ritual; the occasional one requires double the rituals. [He should then say] “Go, go to that great region where dwells the...

<summary>This page details the specific mantras and the systematic procedure for performing a Homa (fire sacrifice) dedicated to Vishnu and his manifestations. It outlines the transition from chanting seed-syllables to making physical offerings in the sacrificial pit, concluding with the practitioner's spiritual union with the Divine.</summary>

By reciting it with reverential faith, a man always destroys his enemies.

<meta>Continuity: This page concludes the philosophical description of the Supreme Being’s nature, which follows the ritual protections and invocations of the preceding chapter. <page-num>35</page-num>
<header>GARUDA PURANAM.</header>

...hold that **Lord Hari** <note>original: "Hari," a name for Vishnu meaning "The Remover" of sin and sorrow</note> alone is the object of meditation. Therefore, listen to this, O great **Lord Ishana** <note>original: "Ishâna," an epithet of Shiva meaning the Ruler or Master</note>.

**Vishnu** <note>The Pervader</note>, the Lord of all, is the destroyer of sins; he is without end, and is devoid of feet or a physical body. He is **Vasudeva** <note>original: "Vâsudeva," the Lord in whom all things dwell</note>, the Sovereign of the universe, and is one and the same as **Brahman** <note>The ultimate, absolute reality in Hindu philosophy</note>. Although he assumes various bodies day by day, in truth, he is entirely free from them. He is beyond the natural functions of the physical form and is untouched by death or decay. Situated within the **six-fold objects** <note>The five senses—sight, sound, smell, taste, touch—plus the mind</note>, he is the Seer, the Hearer, and the Smeller, yet he remains above the reach of the sensory organs. He is devoid of the functions of the senses, is the Creator, and has neither name nor family lineage. The Deity resides in the mind but does not himself possess a mind. He is free from mental faculties, specific discriminative knowledge, and ordinary understanding. He perceives everything through pure intellect and is stationed within it as the Witness of all and the all-knowing; yet at the same time, he is devoid of a limited intellect. He is free from the functions of the intellect, is the totality of all, is present everywhere, and resides in the hearts of all. He is liberated from the **vital breaths** <note>original: "Pranas," the life-forces of the body</note> and is devoid of their actions. But he is the life-principle of all creatures, a soul of perfect peace, and is divorced from all fear. He is shorn of **Egoism** <note>original: "Ahankara," the sense of "I-ness" or self-identity</note> and other material principles, and is devoid of their natural activities. But he is their Witness, their Ordainer, and the very essence of supreme bliss. He is the Witness of the various states of waking, dreaming, and deep dreamless sleep, while he himself exists above them all. He is the **Fourth State** <note>original: "Turiya"</note>,* the great Ordainer, the essence of all directions, and is devoid of **qualities** <note>original: "gunas," the three fundamental attributes of nature: harmony, passion, and inertia</note>. He is emancipated, enlightened, undecaying, all-pervading, all-auspicious, and eternally present within the Self.

By understanding this, those men who meditate on this great **Lord** <note>original: "Isha"</note> attain his very form. There is no need for further ritual actions in this matter. O **Shankara** <note>original: "Shankara," a name for Shiva meaning "The Giver of Joy"</note>, O you of righteous vows,

## CHAPTER XV.

**RUDRA** <note>The Roarer, a name for Shiva</note> said:—O Lord, O **Janārddana** <note>original: "Janārddana," meaning the "Agitator of Men," an epithet for Vishnu as the one who moves or helps all living beings</note>, please describe to me the Great Being. By reciting his name, a person may cross the dreadful ocean of the **cycle of rebirth** <note>original: "Samsara," the continuous cycle of birth, death, and reincarnation which is viewed as a vast and difficult sea to cross</note>.

**HARI** <note>The Remover of sins and sorrow</note> said:—By reciting the thousand names of **Vishnu**, the **Creator** <note>original: "Demiurgus," a term used here for the cosmic architect or the creative power of God</note>, the great **Absolute** <note>original: "Brahman"</note>, the **Indestructible Self** <note>original: "Undecaying Self"</note>, a person attains to **liberation** <note>original: "emancipation" or moksha</note>. O **Lord whose emblem is the bull** <note>original: "bull-emblemed deity," referring to Shiva, whose mount is the bull Nandi</note>, listen with fixed attention; I will describe this sacred and great object of **recitation** <note>original: "Japa," the meditative repetition of a mantra or divine name</note> which destroys all sins.

He is **Vāsudeva** <note>The Lord in whom all things dwell</note>, the great **Vishnu**, the **Dwarf** <note>original: "Vâmana," an incarnation of Vishnu who took the form of a dwarf to humble a powerful demon king</note>, the **Lord of the Vasus** <note>original: "Vasava," usually an epithet of Indra, indicating lordship over the elemental deities called Vasus</note>, the **Dweller** <note>original: "Vasu"</note>, shining brightly like the newly risen sun, and the highly powerful **Balabhadra** <note>The "Auspicious and Strong," the elder brother of Krishna</note>. He bound the **Titan** <note>original: "Asura," a powerful supernatural being often in conflict with the gods</note> **Bali** <note>A great king who had gained immense power through spiritual disciplines</note>; he is the one who knows all, the worshipful knower of the **Sacred Knowledge** <note>original: "Vedas," the foundational scriptures of Hindu tradition</note>, and the inspired poet. He is the creator of the **Sacred Knowledge**, is made of the very form of those scriptures, is the one worthy of being known through them, and is filled with their wisdom. He is the knower of the **Auxiliary Sciences** <note>original: "Vedangas," six branches of knowledge designed to help one correctly pronounce, interpret, and use the Vedas in rituals: phonetics, prosody, grammar, etymology, astronomy, and ceremonial rules</note>, the lord of the **Sacred Knowledge**, the source of strength, and the one who increases the strength of the strong. He is the most excellent cause of ornaments, virtue, creatures, the <term>Vasus</term> <note>A group of eight elemental gods representing aspects of nature and wealth</note>, the <term>Manus</term> <note>The ancestral progenitors of mankind</note>, and of birds. He is the foremost cause of the ascetics, the <term>Yogins</term> <note>Practitioners of spiritual disciplines</note>, the <term>Siddhas</term> <note>Perfected beings possessing supernatural powers</note>, the <term>Yakshas</term> <note>Nature spirits and guardians of treasures</note>, the <term>Kinnaras</term> <note>Celestial musicians with human bodies and horse heads</note>, and <term>Gandharvas</term> <note>Celestial singers and divine beings</note>.

He is the cause of the rivers—both male and female—the oceans, and the trees. He is the cause of <term>Veerudhas</term> <note>Creeping plants and spreading shrubs</note>, the various worlds, the nether regions, and the celestial beings. He is the cause of the serpents, of all auspiciousness, of the beasts, and of everything that exists.

He is identical with the physical body, the organs of sensation, the soul, the intellect, the mind, egoism, consciousness, the state of waking, the state of dreaming sleep, the intellectual principle, and the great soul.

He is identical with the elements of ether, water, and the great soul of the earth and the air. He is the great soul of smell, color, sound, speech, and touch. He permeates, as the great soul, the ears, the skin, the tongue, the nostrils, the hands, the feet, and the organs of generation. He permeates the gods <term>Indra</term>, <term>Brahmā</term> <note>The Creator</note>, <term>Rudra</term> <note>The Roarer, an early form of Shiva</note>, Manu, and the Patriarch <term>Daksha</term> <note>One of the agents of creation and a son of Brahma</note>. The Great One is identical with Truth. He is identical with <term>Isha</term> <note>The Lord</note>, the great soul, Rudra, and the <term>Yati</term> <note>A wandering ascetic who has renounced the world</note> who is well-versed in the knowledge of emancipation. He is energetic and is the very essence of energy itself. He is the holder of the archer's arm-guard and the sword, and he is the destroyer of the <term>Asuras</term> <note>Powerful beings often in conflict with the gods</note>.

He is modest by nature and dedicated to the well-being of the ascetics. <term>Hari</term> <note>A popular name for Vishnu, meaning "the one who takes away" (sins or grief)</note> takes the form of an ascetic and a practitioner of Yoga, and he is the one upon whom the Yogins meditate. He is <term>Shiti</term> <note>Literally "white or black." It is an epithet of Shiva, used here to show the unity of the two deities.</note>. He is perfect knowledge, genius, time, the summer, the rainy season, determination, the year, the giver of liberation, and the destroyer of <term>Moha</term> <note>Spiritual delusion or infatuation that prevents enlightenment</note>.

He stupefies the wicked; he is the sage Mandavya and the "mare-mouthed" fire <note>original: "mare-mouthed" (Badavamukha); a mythical fire at the bottom of the ocean that prevents it from overflowing</note>. He is <term>Sangvartaka</term> <note>The fire of cosmic destruction at the end of an age</note>, the creator of time, and the great sages: Goutama, Bhrigu, Angira, Atri, Vashistha, Pulaha, Pulastya, Kutsa, Yajnavalka, Devala, Vyasa, Parāshara, Sharmada, and Gāngeya. He is the destroyer of miseries; he has beautiful ears and yet exists without them. [He is the] vital air known as <term>Apāna</term> <note>The breath associated with the lower body and elimination</note>, of <term>Vyāna</term> <note>The breath that circulates energy throughout the entire body</note>, of <term>Udāna</term> <note>The upward-moving breath in the throat</note> and of <term>Samāna</term> <note>The balancing breath centered in the abdomen that aids digestion</note>. He is the best master of sound, touch and colour. He is the master of the destruction of the world, the first-born, has a sword in his hand, the plough-share for his weapon, the discus in his hand, the <term>Kundalas</term> <note>Ornate earrings often signifying divine or royal status</note> [on his ears] and the mystic mark <term>Srivatsa</term> <note>A sacred curl of hair or mark on the chest of Vishnu, representing his consort Lakshmi</note> on his breast. He is <term>Prakriti</term> <note>The primordial material nature or the female creative energy of the universe</note>, has the <term>Kaustubha</term> <note>original: "Koustava"; a celebrated jewel that emerged from the churning of the ocean</note> gem on his neck and is clad in a yellow raiment. He has a beautiful face, an ugly face and is without any. He is without end, of endless forms, has beautiful nails and is the handsomest of all the celestials. The lord <term>Vishnu</term> has a beautiful quiver and most resplendent arrows.

He is the destroyer of <term>Hiranyakashipu</term> <note>The demon king slain by the Narasimha (man-lion) avatar</note>, the grinder of <term>Hiranyaksha</term> <note>The demon who submerged the earth, slain by the Varaha (boar) avatar</note>, the killer of <term>Putanā</term> <note>A demoness who tried to kill the infant Krishna with poisoned milk</note> and <term>Bhāskarānta</term>. He is the grinder of <term>Keshin</term> <note>A horse-demon killed by Krishna</note>, and <term>Mushtika</term> <note>A demonic wrestler in the service of King Kamsa</note>. He is the killer of the demon <term>Kansa</term> <note>The tyrant king of Mathura and Krishna’s uncle</note>, of <term>Chānura</term> and <term>Aristha</term> <note>A bull-demon killed by Krishna</note>. He is fond of <term>Akrura</term> <note>A kinsman and devotee of Krishna</note>. He is without wickedness, is wily and is adored by those who are fond of simplicity. He is the destroyer of the lordly powers, is himself endued with them, the sun and the Lord himself. He is <term>Uddhava</term> <note>Krishna’s close friend and counselor</note>, <term>Uddhava</term>'s lord, and is being meditated on by <term>Uddhava</term>.

He is the holder of the discus, is fickle and is devoid of the moveable and immoveable properties. He is egoism, determination, mental faculties, the sky, earth, water, air, eye, ear, tongue, nose, palate, hand, foot, waist and the organ of generation. He is <term>Shankara</term> <note>A name for Shiva, demonstrating the ultimate unity of the two deities</note>, the giver of auspiciousness, the giver of endurance; and he forgives men. He is fond of his votaries <note>devoted followers</note> and is their protector. He is endued with reverential faith and himself increases it. He is lauded by his votaries, devoted to them and gives fame; and he multiplies it.

He has a thousand feet. He is the thousand-headed physician who opens the gate of emancipation <note>liberation from the cycle of birth and death</note>. [He] has a beautiful head-gear and a beautiful neck. He is daily sought by people for help; he is enlightened, emancipated, and the holder of all forms. He is the destroyer of <term>Khara</term> and <term>Dushana</term> <note>Demons defeated by Rama in the Dandaka forest</note> and the grinder of <term>Rāvana</term> <note>The ten-headed antagonist of the Ramayana</note>. He is the husband of <term>Sita</term> and the prosperous <term>Bharata</term> <note>Rama's brother</note>. He is the killer of the victor of <term>Kumbhendra</term> <note>A reference to Indrajit, the son of Ravana who had defeated Indra</note>, the grinder of <term>Kumbhakarna</term> <note>Ravana's giant brother</note>, and the destroyer of the killers of men and celestials. He is the destroyer of the wicked <term>Asuras</term> <note>original: "Asuras"; a class of powerful beings often in conflict with the gods</note> and the enemy of <term>Shamvara</term> <note>A demon defeated by Pradyumna</note>. He is the destroyer of the demon <term>Naraka</term> as well as the three-headed demon. He broke down the twin trees <term>Yamala</term> and <term>Arjuna</term> <note>A reference to a miracle where the infant Krishna uprooted two cursed trees</note> and always helps those in a state of asceticism. He plays on the musical instrument and is himself the instrument. He is enlightened and the giver of boons. He is the substance and is fond of substances. He is <term>Soura</term> <note>Relating to the Sun or the solar deity Surya</note> and the destroyer of Time and is incapable of being cut. He is the sages <term>Agastya</term>, <term>Devala</term>, and <term>Nārada</term>, and is fond of Nārada. He is <term>Prāna</term>, <term>Apāna</term>, and <term>Vyāna</term> <note>The vital breaths or life-forces that regulate the body</note>, and the qualities of <term>Rajas</term> <note>Passion and activity; the OCR incorrectly identifies this as darkness</note>, <term>Sattwa</term> <note>Purity and goodness</note>, and <term>Tamas</term> <note>Ignorance and inertia; the OCR identifies this as darkness</note>. He is beyond <term>Tamas</term> (ignorance). He is <term>Udāna</term> and <term>Samāna</term> <note>The remaining two of the five vital life-forces</note>. He is the medicine and he is the medical man.

He is perpetually and universally the same. His form is transparent, and yet he is devoid of any form. He is devoid of the organs of vision and speech, of hands, of fat, and of the organs of generation and excretion. He is devoid of great asceticism <note>In his ultimate state, God is beyond the need for spiritual practice or effort</note>. He is devoid of perception, intellect, consciousness, and the vital airs: <term>Prāna</term>, <term>Apāna</term>, <term>Vyāna</term>, <term>Udāna</term>, and <term>Samāna</term>.

He is devoid of ether and fire, water and earth. He is devoid of sound, touch, and all colors. He is devoid of carnal passion and other inferior appetites. He is shorn of grief and the power of speech. He is devoid of the quality of <term>Rajas</term> and the six-fold deviations from the natural state <note>The "Shad-vikara" or six changes of all living things: birth, existence, growth, change, decay, and death</note>. He is the gross matter. He is clever, the leader of the strong, and the one who stirs all things into motion. He agitates <term>Prakriti</term> <note>original: "Prakriti"; the primal, unmanifested matter of nature</note>, <term>Mahat</term> <note>original: "Mahat"; the great intellectual principle or first stage of cosmic evolution</note>, the elements, the intellect, the organs of sense, and the objects of those senses. He is the one who moves both <term>Brahma</term> <note>The creator god</note> and <term>Rudra</term> <note>A fierce form of Shiva, the destroyer</note>. He is beyond the range of sight and hearing. Skin cannot touch him. He is the tortoise <note>Likely referring to the Kurma avatar who supported the world during the churning of the ocean</note>. The tongue cannot perceive him. He is beyond the reach of smell and speech. The hands and feet cannot reach him. <term>Hari</term> <note>A name for Vishnu meaning "He who takes away" (sins or sorrows)</note> is beyond the reach of mental and intellectual perception and understanding. He is only within the comprehension of the sense of the "I" (ego) and the inner mental faculties.

He holds the conch-shell in his <unclear>hand</unclear>, is undecaying, and also holds the club and the <term>Shranga</term> <note>original: "Shranga"; more commonly spelled "Sharnga," the celestial bow of Vishnu</note> bow in his hands. He is dark-blue, the very image of knowledge, and the consumer of his enemies. He is within the range of the <unclear>knowledge</unclear> of the ascetics; he is endowed with all knowledge and knows everything through his own wisdom. He is understood by knowledge and reveals the awareness of all objects of knowledge. He is the soul, the object of mental perception, the creator of the world, and its destroyer. He is <term>Govinda</term> <note>A name meaning "Protector of Cows" or "Finder of the Earth"</note>, the lord of the cattle and the giver of happiness to the cow-herds. He is the protector of cattle, the master <unclear>of</unclear> cattle, <term>Gomati</term> <note>A name associated with cows or a sacred river</note>, and <term>Godhara</term> <note>One who supports the earth or cattle</note>. He is <term>Upendra</term> <note>A name meaning "Indra's younger brother," referring to the Vamana avatar</note>, <term>Nrisimha</term> <note>The Man-Lion incarnation</note>, <term>Shouri</term> <note>A descendant of Shura, a name for Krishna/Vishnu</note>, <term>Janārddana</term> <note>The one who is prayed to by people for liberation</note>, <term>Araneya</term> <note>One born from the friction of the fire-sticks (Arani)</note>, <term>Vrihatbhanu</term> <note>The "Great Sun" or one possessing great brilliance</note>, and is highly radiant. He is <term>Dāmodara</term> <note>A name for Krishna, who was tied with a rope (dama) around his belly (udara) by his mother</note>, the three-fold nature of time <note>Past, present, and future</note>, aware of all time, and yet existing outside of time. He is the three-fold <term>Sandhya</term> <note>The prayer periods at dawn, noon, and dusk</note>, he is the <term>Dwapara</term> and <term>Treta</term> <note>Two of the four great ages (Yugas) of the world</note> eras, and the creator of both the people and the paths they walk. He is powerful and carries a rod in his hand. He holds both the single and the triple rods <note>Referring to the Ekadanda and Tridanda staffs carried by different orders of wandering monks</note>. He is the division of the <term>Samaveda</term> <note>The Veda of Melodies and Chants</note>, its various methods, the form of the <term>Saman</term> <note>The specific hymns or songs of the Samaveda</note> itself, and the one who chants them. He is well-versed in the <term>Atharvan Veda</term> <note>The Veda dealing with rituals, spells, and everyday life</note> and is its chief teacher. He is the <term>Rik</term> <note>The individual verses</note> of the <term>Rik Veda</term> <note>The oldest Veda, containing hymns of praise</note> and is stationed within it. He is the reader of the <term>Yayush</term> <note>The "Yajus" or sacrificial formulas</note>, the <term>Yayurveda</term> <note>The Yajur Veda, the Veda of sacrificial rituals</note> itself, and is expert in its ceremonies. He has one foot, many feet, beautiful feet, a thousand feet, four feet, and two feet <note>A reference to the Purusha Sukta, describing the Divine as manifesting in all creatures—bipeds, quadrupeds, and the infinite forms of the universe</note>.

It lists several of his names and confirms his role as the master of time and the sustainer of the world through the cow-herd imagery.</summary>

-># GARUDA PURANAM.<-
<page-num>44</page-num>

The powerful lord is like the <term>Smriti</term> <note>original: "Smriti"; a category of Hindu texts based on tradition and memory, including law codes and epics</note> and <term>Nyaya</term> <note>original: "Nyaya"; the school of logic and reasoning, one of the six systems of Indian philosophy</note>. He is a <term>Sanyasin</term> <note>a person who has renounced worldly life for spiritual pursuits</note> and embodies the four stages of life, namely: a <term>Brahmacharin</term> <note>a student of sacred knowledge</note>, a <term>Grihasta</term> <note>a householder</note>, a <term>Vānaprasta</term> <note>one retired into the forest</note>, and a <term>Bhikshsu</term> <note>a wandering monk living on alms</note>. He is the four <term>Varnas</term> <note>the social classes of Vedic society</note>, including the <term>Brahmanas</term> <note>priests and scholars</note>, the <term>Kshatryas</term> <note>warriors and rulers</note>, the <term>Vaishyas</term> <note>merchants and farmers</note>, and the <term>Shudras</term> <note>laborers and artisans</note>. He gives good character, is filled with the same, and is entirely free of any bad character. He is spiritual liberation itself, engaged in divine meditation; he is the hymn of praise, the one who praises, and the one who is worshipped. He is the one worthy of worship, the speech, the meaning of speech, and the speaker. He is the knower, the grammar, the word, and is fully mastery of all language.

He is within the reach of words, lives in holy places, is the holy place itself, and possesses full knowledge of all sacred sites. He resides in every pilgrimage destination. He is <term>Sankhya</term> <note>a philosophical system based on dualism and enumeration</note>, <term>Nirukta</term> <note>the science of etymology, particularly of Vedic words</note>, and the deity who presides over them. He is *<term>Pranava</term>,* <note>original: "Pranava"; the sacred syllable *Om*, representing the Hindu triad of Brahma (creator), Vishnu (protector), and Shiva (destroyer)</note> the lord of the *<term>Pranava</term>*, and is worshipped through the *<term>Pranava</term>*. He is <term>Gayatri</term> <note>a highly sacred verse from the Rig Veda dedicated to the sun-god, recited daily by the faithful</note> and the holder of the divine club. He lives within the <term>Shalagrama</term> <note>a sacred fossilized stone found in the Gandaki River, worshipped as a form of Vishnu</note> and is the <term>Shalagrama</term> stone itself. He resides in the waters and rests in the state of <term>Yoga</term>, reclining upon the serpent <term>Sesha</term> <note>the giant celestial serpent who supports the world</note> and the sacred <term>Kushâ</term> grass. He is the earth, the very act of doing, the cause behind it, and the one who supports the world. He is the Patriarch <note>likely referring to Prajapati, the progenitor of all beings</note> and the eternal being. He is the object of all desire and the one who creates desire throughout the universe. He is the Supreme Sovereign, the sun, and the heavens; he is seated upon a chariot and is the very strength of the charioteer. He is wealth, the giver of riches, the blessed one, and is ever devoted to the welfare of the <term>Yadavas</term> <note>the lineage or clan of Krishna</note>. He is the beloved friend of <term>Arjuna</term> and he is both <term>Arjuna</term> and <term>Bhima</term> <note>two of the heroic Pandava brothers from the Mahabharata epic</note>. His power is overwhelming, and he is the master of all the...

## CHAPTER XVI.
---

**RUDRA** <note>a fierce form of the god Shiva</note> said:—O holder of the conch-shell, discus, and club, I ask you to describe again the <term>Dhyana</term> <note>the practice of concentrated meditation or visualization</note> of the Deity—the Lord Vishnu, the pure and impersonal self.

**HARI** <note>a name for Vishnu meaning "The Remover of Sorrows"</note> said:—Hear, O Rudra, the meditation upon Hari, which destroys the tree of the <term>transmigratory series</term> <note>the cycle of birth, death, and rebirth known as Samsara</note>. This meditation has never been seen before; it is all-extending, eternal, undecaying, and ever-present...

and everywhere and consists only in the thought "I am <term>Brahma</term>" <note>the ultimate, impersonal reality or absolute spirit of the universe</note>. Meditate on him as the root of the entire world, the lord of all, the Great <term>Demiurgus</term> <note>the architect or creator of the physical world</note>, as stationed in the hearts of all creatures, as the great lord of all creatures. He is the container of all, having none to contain him, and is the cause of all causes. He does not come in contact with worldly attachments, is emancipated, and is being meditated on by emancipated <term>Yogins</term> <note>practitioners of yoga who have achieved spiritual liberation</note>. He is without the gross body, the eyes, organs of vitality, the action of vital airs, the organs of generation and excretion, the organs of sense, the mind, the action of mind, intellect, mental faculties, egoism, the action of intellect, the vital airs, <term>Prāna</term> <note>the upward-moving life breath</note>, <term>Apāna</term> <note>the downward-moving life breath</note> and their actions.

**HARI** <note>an epithet of Vishnu, meaning "The Remover of Sorrows"</note> said:—I will describe again the adoration of the sun which had been related formerly unto **Bhrigu** <note>one of the seven great sages (Saptarishis) in Vedic tradition</note>.

> **Om** <note>the primordial sacred syllable</note> salutation unto **Khakholka** <note>literally "the fireball of the sky," a name for the Sun</note>.

This is the principal <term>mantram</term> <note>a sacred utterance or prayer</note> of the sun affording emancipation and objects of enjoyment.

> **Om** salutation unto God **Khakholka**. **Om** unto rays <note>original: "ta, ta"</note>, salutation unto the head. **Om** unto knowledge, salutation unto the tuft of hair on the head. **Om** unto him of thousand <note>rays</note>, <note>original: "ta, ta"</note>, salutation unto the amulet.

> **Om** salutation unto the master of all light. <note>original: "Ta, Ta"</note>, salutation unto the weapon. **Om**, burn, burn, burn, burn, <note>original: "ta, ta"</note>, salutation.

This is the fiery <term>mantram</term> of the sun destructive of the sin.

> **Om**, let us know the Sun, let us meditate on the Source of the Universe; may that Sun inspire our intellect <note>original: "Om Adityāya, Vidmahe, Vishwabhavaya dhimahi, Tanna Suryye Prachodayat"; this is the Surya Gayatri mantra</note>.

The worshipper should perform the *Sakalikarana* rite <note>a ritual of "making whole" where the deity's energy is placed into the worshipper's body</note> with this <term>Gayatri</term> <note>a specific rhythmic prayer or meter</note> of the sun. He should worship **Dharma** <note>the god of righteousness</note> in the east, **Yama** <note>the god of death and justice</note> in the south, **Dandanāyaka** <note>the "Staff-Bearer," an attendant of the Sun</note> and **Vaivarna** <note>an attendant of the Sun, likely related to the god of the sea, Varuna</note> in the north, dark-blue, tawny and other colors in north-east and north-west, the holder of thunder-bolt in the south-west and the earth and sky in the north-west.

**Om** <note>the primordial sacred sound</note> salutation unto the moon, the lord of stars. **Om** salutation unto **Mars** <note>original: "egoism," referring to the deity Mangala</note>, the son of the earth. **Om**, salutation unto **Mercury** <note>original: "Budha"</note>, the son of **Soma** <note>the Moon</note>. **Om** salutation unto the lord of speech <note>the planet Jupiter, or Brihaspati</note>, the master of all forms of learning. **Om** salutation unto **Bhrigu's** son, the great saint **Shukra** <note>the planet Venus</note>. **Om** salutation unto **Shani** (**Saturn**), the son of the sun. **Om** salutation unto **Rāhu** <note>the celestial body responsible for eclipses</note>. **Om** salutation unto **Ketu** <note>the comet or descending node of the moon</note>.

O lord! O thou endued with immeasurable rays! O lord of the entire world! O thou carried by seven horses! O thou having four arms! O thou, the giver of great supernatural powers! Having invoked the sun-god with this **mantram** <note>a sacred prayer or chant</note>, he should perform the **Visarjana** <note>original: "life-destroying rite," referring to the ritual dismissal or "sending away" of the deity's presence at the conclusion of the ceremony</note> with the following prayer.

**Om** salutation unto the sun endued with six lordly powers, of a thousand rays. Go happily to return again.

<meta>This page continues the ritual instructions for planetary and solar worship, moving from the general salutations of the previous page to the specific geometric arrangement (mandala) and directional placement of deities within a sacred space.</meta>

-># GARUDA PURANAM.<-
<page-num>50</page-num>

In a purified place, a worshipper should draw the figure of a lotus with eight petals and pericarps <note>the seed vessel or center of the lotus</note>. Then making the **Gesture of Invocation** <term>Avahani mudra<note>original: "Avahani mudra," a specific arrangement of the fingers used to formally invite a deity to be present at a ritual</note></term>, he should invoke **Hari** <note>a popular name for the god Vishnu, meaning "the Remover of Sorrows"</note> there.

He should place in the middle the diagram form of the sun and sprinkle it with water. He should place the "heart" of the deity in the quarter presided over by the fire-god <note>the South-east direction</note>. He should place the "head" in the north-east and the "tuft of hair" <note>referring to the *shikha*, a traditional lock of hair</note> in the south-west. Having his mind fixed in concentration, he should assign **Dharma** <note>righteousness or cosmic order personified</note> to the quarter presided over by **Indra** <term>Purandara<note>an epithet for Indra, the King of the Gods, who rules the East</note></term>. He should place his eyes in the north-west and his weapon in the corner presided over by **Varuna** <note>the god of the oceans and the West</note>. He should place **Soma** <note>the Moon</note> in the north-east and **Mars** <term>Lohita<note>literally "The Red One," referring to the planet Mars</note></term> in the quarter belonging to **Indra**. He should place Soma’s son <note>Mercury, known as Budha</note> in the east and **Jupiter** <term>Vrihaspati</term> in the south. He should place the **teacher of the Titans** <term>Shukra<note>the planet Venus, who serves as the preceptor for the Danavas or demons</note></term> in the south-west and **Saturn** <term>Shani</term> in the corner presided over by Varuna. He should place **Ketu** <note>the descending node of the moon</note> in the north-west and **Rahu** <note>the ascending node of the moon</note> in the quarter presided over by **Kubera** <term>Kuvera<note>the god of wealth who rules the North</note></term>.

In the second apartment <note>an outer enclosure or ring of the diagram</note>, he should adore the twelve suns, namely: **Bhaga**, **Surya**, **Aryama**, **Mitra**, **Varuna**, **Savita**, **Dhata**, the highly powerful **Vivaswan**, **Twashta**, **Pusha**, and **Indra**. The twelfth is **Vishnu**. In the quarters beginning with the east, a man, filled with reverence, should adore Indra and other deities: **Jaya**, **Vijaya**, **Jayanti**, **Aparajita**, **Sesha**, **Vasuki**, and other **Serpent Deities** <term>Nagas</term>.

<summary>This page details the ritual construction of a sacred lotus diagram (mandala) used to invoke Vishnu and arrange various planetary and solar deities according to their specific cardinal directions. It concludes with the listing of the twelve aspects of the Sun and several protective guardian spirits and serpent deities.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the planetary diagram worship described in the previous chapter to the specific ritual adoration of Mritunjaya, the Conqueror of Death. It introduces a powerful three-syllable mantra and details the meditative visualizations and ritual components required for the practitioner to achieve spiritual liberation and protection from death.</meta>

-># GARUDA PURANAM.<-
<page-num>51</page-num>

->## CHAPTER XVIII.<-

**Suta** <note>a traditional storyteller in the Puranas, often depicted addressing an audience of sages in the Naimisha forest</note> said:—I will describe the <term>Archnā<note>original: "Archanā," the formal ritual of offering adoration and worship to a deity</note></term> of **Mritunjaya** <term>the victor of death<note>an aspect of the god Shiva, invoked specifically for healing and the prevention of untimely death</note></term> as it was narrated to **Kashyapa** by **Garuda**. This practice brings redemption, is considered holy, and is seen as identical with all the deities combined.

First, the sacred syllable **Om** should be placed, then the sound **jumkara** <note>the seed-syllable "Juṃ"</note>, and thirdly the **Virsarga** <note>the "Saḥ" sound, characterized by the two dots or aspiration in Sanskrit</note>. This <term>mantram<note>a sacred utterance or incantation believed to have spiritual and psychological power</note></term> destroys death and poverty. This great incantation of three letters is the lord of ambrosia <note>the nectar of immortality, or *Amrita*</note>. By reciting it, people become freed from death and all manner of sins. By reciting it one hundred times, one reaps the same spiritual merit as reciting the **Vedas** or performing sacrifices at the most sacred sites. 

By reciting it one hundred and eight times during the three periods of junction <note>the *Sandhyas*: dawn, noon, and dusk</note>, one defeats Death and his enemies. The worshipper should meditate on the Lord of Ambrosia, who is seated upon a white lotus. He is the giver of boons, shown with one hand in a gesture of protection and two other hands holding jars full of nectar. He should also visualize the Goddess of Ambrosia as if she were a part of his own body; she is sweet-spoken like the nectar itself, holding a pitcher in her right hand and a lotus in her left. 

Whoever recites this eight thousand times at the three junctions for one full month becomes freed from the frailties of old age, death, and leprosy; he defeats his enemies and brings peace to all living creatures. 

The [true] worshipper is one who understands:
*   the site [of a temple]
*   the placing [of an idol]
*   the suppression of vital airs <note>referring to *Pranayama*, or yogic breath control</note>
*   the appearance and the temple
*   the water for washing feet and rinsing the mouth
*   the water for bathing
*   <term>Arghya<note>a formal offering of water, often mixed with flowers and rice, to a guest or deity</note></term>
*   <term>Aguru<note>fragrant eaglewood or aloe used for incense</note></term>
*   pastes, lamps, raiments <note>clothing</note>, and ornaments
*   edibles, drinks, and drinking water
*   <term>Matra<note>the rhythm or measure of the chants</note></term>
*   <term>Mudra<note>sacred hand gestures that seal or direct energy during ritual</note></term>
*   recitation, meditation, and gifts
*   oblation <note>offerings made into a sacred fire</note>
*   the chanting of glories and playing on musical instruments
*   singing and dancing
*   <term>Nyāsa<note>the ritual practice of "placing" specific mantras or deities onto different parts of the worshipper's own body</note></term>
*   yoga and circumambulation <note>walking clockwise around a sacred object</note>
*   bowing and the chanting of incantations
*   offering clarified butter <note>ghee</note> and eulogies
*   the <term>Visarjana<note>the final rite of the ritual where the deity is formally requested to depart from the idol or diagram; the OCR refers to this as "life-destroying" in the sense of ending the ritual presence</note></term> 

This is the adoration performed with the six ingredients that flow in order from the mouth of the Great Deity.

<summary>This chapter details the worship of Mritunjaya (Shiva) through a specific three-syllable mantra and visualization of the deity of immortality. It lists the comprehensive ritual requirements for a practitioner to achieve health, victory over enemies, and liberation from death.</summary>
<keywords>Mritunjaya, mantra, Om Jum Sah, ambrosia, Amrita, Shiva worship, ritual components, Nyasa, Visarjana, immortality</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the ritual instructions for the worship of Mritunjaya (the Conqueror of Death), moving from the recitation of the primary mantra to the specific physical and meditative preparations of the ritual space and the practitioner's own body to achieve union with the divine.</meta>

-># GARUDA PURANAM.<-
<page-num>52</page-num>

The <term>Arghya<note>a ritual offering of water, milk, or herbs as a sign of hospitality to a deity</note></term>, <term>Padya<note>water offered specifically for washing the feet of the deity</note></term>, and other offerings should be fanned with a piece of cloth, then purified with *Armor incantations* <note>mantras intended to create a spiritual shield around the practitioner and the ritual items</note>, followed by the rite of <term>Nectarization<note>original: "Amriti-Karana," the ritual process of converting offerings into immortal nectar</note></term>. Then the <term>Foundational Powers<note>original: "Adhara Saktis," female deities described in the original footnotes as presiding over various ritual articles and the foundation of the universe</note></term> should be adored, and <term>Breath Control<note>original: "Prānāyāma," the practice of regulating or suppressing the vital breaths to steady the mind</note></term> should be practiced while in the ritual seat. Then the purificatory rite of the <term>Pinda<note>originally referring to balls of food offered to the spirits of the departed ancestors</note></term> should be performed. 

Then, with the <term>Fire<note>original: "Agni"</note></term> and other incantations, the worshipper should meditate on the self as being identical with the deity. He should then make a ritual assignment of the hands and other limbs and afterwards adore the self in the form of light stationed in the lotus of the heart. He should then throw shining flowers on the idol or the altar. For the adoration of the "door of the self," the <term>Foundational Powers</term> should be worshipped. And bringing one’s self near the deity, he worships his divine family. For the adoration of the six <term>Limbs<note>original: "Angas," the various parts of the body or components of the ritual</note></term>, the directions should be divided. <term>Dharma<note>the god of righteousness and duty</note></term> and other gods, <term>Shakra<note>another name for Indra, the king of the gods</note></term> and other celestials, the various members of their families and their weapons, the cosmic cycles, and the <term>Units of Time<note>original: "Muhurttas," divisions of the day roughly equivalent to 48 minutes</note></term> should be adored. This worship yields both worldly enjoyment and spiritual liberation. He should first adore the <term>Mother Goddesses<note>original: "Matrikas"</note></term>, the <term>Divine Attendants<note>original: "Ganas"</note></term>, <term>Nandi and Ganga</term>, <term>Great Time<note>original: "Mahakāla," an aspect of Shiva as the destroyer of time</note></term>, the goddess of the <term>Yamuna</term> river, and the <term>Threshold Spirits<note>original: "Dehalya"</note></term>.

**Om**, salutations unto **Bhairava** <note>the fierce or "terrible" manifestation of Shiva</note>, the lord of nectar. <term>Thus<note>original: "Evam"</note></term>, **Om**, **Jum**, **Sah** <note>these are seed-syllables of the Mritunjaya mantra described on the previous page</note>, salutations unto the sun. In this way, adorations should be offered to **Shiva**, **Krishna**, **Brahma**, **Gana Chandikā**, **Saraswati**, **Mahalakshmi**, and others.
